```text
  clean     mismatches=3,422   worst chunk (49,48): 1,008
  poisoned  mismatches=6,952   worst chunk (50,48):   957
  NW-CORNER-POISON FIRED
```

It more than doubles, and the worst chunk *moves*. The lump really is routed
through the trunk placer, so the brief is sound and the next agent does not
have to discover that for itself.

### THE TREE MOVED UNDER THIS MEASUREMENT, AND THE CLEAN RUN IS HOW IT WAS CAUGHT

The clean arm of that control reads **3,422** over the five chunks where the
census run, taken two hours earlier on the same window with the same order,
read **3,440**. Eighteen blocks, and the generator was supposed to be
identical.

It was not. `worldgen-blue-ice-iceberg` was **restacked** while this work was
in flight — acb99d72 became 719ecda2, the same three commits rebased onto a
newer `main` — and the newer `main` carries another agent's fluid-stage repair
(`crates/rustmc-worldgen/src/stage/fluid.rs`, +571 lines, the 58 closed
fluid-`level` blocks) along with carver and post-process changes. So the
census above was taken on acb99d72's generator and this branch now sits on a
different one.

This is the standing hazard — *a restack moves a branch's own baseline* —
arriving in the one form that is easy to miss: **not two agents disagreeing,
but one agent disagreeing with itself two hours apart.** The eighteen blocks
were only visible because the poison control happened to re-measure a
quantity that had already been measured. Every figure in this section is
therefore re-taken on the rebased tree before it is registered, and the
provenance names 719ecda2 and not acb99d72.

## 46. `-Dmax.bg.threads=1` sets a pool's PARALLELISM, not its thread count, and does not make a nether harvest reproducible (2026-08-18)

Branch `nether-biome-census`. Six vanilla harvests of one seed and one window
— **seed 20260722, nether, centre chunk (64,64), r=7, chunks 57..71,
`--ybox 0 127`, `GEN_ORDER=rows`, 7,372,800 blocks** — three of them under
`-Dmax.bg.threads=1` and three under the default pool. Box: threadripper,
`nproc` 24, load average 10.7–17.8 throughout (four corpus runs were queued on
the same box; every harvest's load is in its log line).

The clamp was expected to zero the disagreement. It does not.

```text
  clamped pairs      (3 runs, 3 pairs)      34    438    460
  default-pool pairs (3 runs, 3 pairs)      75    134    151
```

**The clamped spread is wider than the unclamped one and its worst pair is
three times the unclamped worst.** This is not a clamp with a small residual;
it is a knob that does not control the quantity.

### Why, out of the pinned jar

`Util.<clinit>` is `BACKGROUND_EXECUTOR = makeExecutor("Main")`, and
`makeExecutor` is

```text
  int n = maxAllowedExecutorThreads();          // Mth.clamp(procs-1, 1, getMaxThreads())
  if (n <= 0)  MoreExecutors.newDirectExecutorService()
  else         new ForkJoinPool(n, <factory naming "Worker-"+name+"-"+i>,
                                <handler>, /*asyncMode=*/ true)
```

`javap -p -c -constants net/minecraft/util/Util.class`, pinned
`server-26.2.jar`. The first argument to `ForkJoinPool`'s constructor is its
**target parallelism**, not a maximum thread count: a `ForkJoinPool` spawns
*compensation* workers when an existing worker blocks, so that the configured
parallelism can still be met. Setting `max.bg.threads=1` therefore asks for
parallelism 1 and gets a pool that will still run two tasks at once.

**Measured, not inferred.** Boot the pinned jar with and without the flag and
count the threads the factory actually made — the factory names them from an
`AtomicInteger` starting at 1, so the highest suffix is the count:

```text
  -Dmax.bg.threads=1     Worker-Main-1, Worker-Main-2         2 threads
  default                Worker-Main-1 .. Worker-Main-23     23 threads
  neither boot logged a "Wrong ... property value" warning
```

**§1 of this file says "With `-Dmax.bg.threads=1` the live server has exactly
one `Worker-Main` thread (`ps -T`)". That is wrong and this supersedes it.**
There are two. The knob did turn — 2 against 23 is a 91.3% reduction and no
property warning was logged — but the residual concurrency is not zero, and in
a `ForkJoinPool` two workers are all it takes for two chunks to reach FEATURES
in an order that is not fixed.

### So the clamp is not a determinism control, and the r=2 overworld result does not transfer

The clamp was adopted on the strength of an r=2 **overworld** window — 25
chunks, 884,736 blocks — where four clamped runs gave six pairs all zero. That
result stands and this does not contradict it. What it does contradict is the
generalisation: **a window over which a clamp reads zero is a window with
nothing in it for the residual concurrency to move**, and the nether window
here is 8.3x larger in blocks and holds a `count_on_every_layer` family that
reads a neighbour chunk before writing (§1 names it). Same rule as a floor
belonging to the pair of runs that produced it: a clamp result belongs to the
window it was taken on.

### And what the three clamped runs actually produced

Not two worlds and not one. The six harvests fall into **three** groups, by
the `basalt_deltas` spelling of chunks (68..70, 71):

```text
  group X   clamp-a, clamp-b
  group Y   clamp-c, open-a, open-c
  group Z   open-b
```

`clamp-c` is grouped with two default-pool runs and against the other two
clamped ones. **The clamp does not even select which group you land in**,
which is the sharpest available statement that it is not the control. The
cross-group basalt distances are 255 (X↔Y), 294 (X↔Z) and 39 (Y↔Z); within
group Y the runs still differ by 21..78 blocks of `soul_sand_valley`, so
grouping is not agreement either.

### What this changes elsewhere

* **Every floor in this repo remains an unclamped floor, and that is now a
  statement with nothing behind it in this dimension** — there is no clamped
  floor to compare it to, because clamping does not produce one.
* Do **not** re-run the configuration ladder in §2 hoping for a lower nether
  floor. §2 already concluded "the levers are exhausted"; this adds the reason
  the last lever never worked, which §2 could not state because it read the
  parallelism argument as a thread count.
* A clamped harvest is still a *legitimate* vanilla output and costs the same
  45 s. It is simply not a more reproducible one.

---

## 47. A nether floor is a statement about the window's biome mix: 15.6% of the r=16 window carries 98.9% of it (2026-08-18)

Branch `nether-biome-census`, instrument `region-differ diff --by-biome`,
which charges every compared position and every mismatch to the biome
**vanilla** records there.

Until this was measured, a nether floor was one scalar, and two windows'
floors were compared as though they described the same thing. They do not.

### The r=16 window, vanilla against vanilla

Seed 20260722, nether, centre chunk (64,64), chunks 48..80, `--ybox 0 127`,
35,684,352 blocks, references `gen-wide-20260722-r16-nether-*`, all
`gen_order=rows` except the two `rr-*`:

```text
  pair                    total     basalt_deltas   soul_sand_valley   nether_wastes
  a  vs b               377,949           373,863              4,048              38
  a  vs c               368,933           365,450              3,444              39
  a  vs d               366,675           362,374              4,260              41
  b  vs c               402,103           398,660              3,442               1
  e  vs f               318,043           313,045              4,960              38
  a  vs e               345,174                 —              4,427              38
  rr-a vs rr-b          348,260           342,496              5,153             611
  a vs rr-a  (ORDER)    695,298           681,108             13,946             244

  window share          100%      15.59%              84.33%             0.08%
                                  5,562,368 blocks    30,093,312         28,672
```

**15.59% of the window carries 98.4–99.1% of the floor in every same-order
pair.** Per block, vanilla's own irreproducibility is **500x denser** in
`basalt_deltas` than in `soul_sand_valley` — 6.72% of positions against one
in 7,434 on the `a`/`b` pair.

So the per-biome floors, over six same-order pairs, are:

```text
  basalt_deltas       313,045 .. 398,660     5.63% .. 7.17% of its blocks
  soul_sand_valley      3,442 ..   4,960     1 position in 8,742 .. 6,067
  nether_wastes             1 ..      41
```

Those are three different instruments, not one instrument's spread. A defect
of ours worth 4,000 blocks is invisible in `basalt_deltas` and a headline in
`soul_sand_valley`, and an aggregate figure over a window mixes them in
whatever proportion that window happens to hold.

### Which is why two published nether numbers were never comparable

`docs/PARITY.md` §5 reports 8,472 mismatches on one r=7 window and 39,941 on
another and says the second is not a regression because it is a different
biome. That was the right conclusion from a partial reason. The full reason is
that the second window's **floor** is 800x the first's, so the two numbers are
not measurements of the same quantity at all.

And the biome labels in that section are themselves wrong, measured here
rather than assumed. Both r=7 windows, `--ybox 0 127`, 7,372,800 blocks:

```text
  seed 20260722 c(64,64)     soul_sand_valley 99.33%   basalt_deltas 0.67%
  seed 20260722 c(100,100)   soul_sand_valley 100%
  seed 31415926 c(64,64)     nether_wastes 42.17%   basalt_deltas 29.44%
                             soul_sand_valley 28.39%
```

§5 calls the two 20260722 windows "100% `soul_sand_valley`" — true of the
second, false of the first, whose 0.67% of `basalt_deltas` is **49,152 blocks
carrying 55–58% of that window's vanilla-pair disagreement**. And it calls the
31415926 window "basalt deltas", where basalt is its second biome by area and
`nether_wastes` — a biome the same section calls unmeasured — is its first.

### The rule

**Quote a nether figure with its biome decomposition or not at all.** An
aggregate is a weighted average whose weights are the window's biome areas,
and nobody reading it knows those weights. This is the nether's form of the
existing rule that a floor belongs to the pair of runs that produced it: a
nether *exactness* belongs to the biome mix that produced it.

`region-differ diff --by-biome` prints window share beside residual share for
exactly this reason. A biome that is 84% of the window and 1% of the floor is
the useful null, and it is invisible in either column alone.

---

## 48. The overworld's three published windows are one map location, hold twelve of fifty-five biomes, and are exhausted in every one of them (2026-08-18)

Section 47 decomposed a nether window because an aggregate exactness over a
mixed window is a weighted average whose weights nobody reading it knows. The
same instrument turned on the overworld answers a larger question than it was
built for, because the overworld's windows are narrower than the nether's in a
way that no figure recorded.

**Every published overworld figure is centre chunk (64,64) at r=16.**
`ci/figures.toml` registers 41 of them, on seeds 20260722, 12345 and 31415926.
`docs/PARITY.md` section 2's two narrower rows — overworld chunks 57..71 and
chunks 59..69 — are sub-squares of that identical centre. Three points on the
map, all at the same coordinates, and the great majority of this project's
published parity rests on them.

### 48.1 What is in them

`region-differ diff X X --by-biome` — a reference against itself, zero
mismatches and a complete decomposition for free. Measured on the published
references over their own published bboxes:

```text
  20260722 r=16 c(64,64)          12345 r=16 c(64,64)
    savanna           42.74%        frozen_ocean      78.70%
    lukewarm_ocean    39.34%        snowy_plains       8.43%
    river              7.44%        snowy_beach        8.30%
    beach              6.48%        frozen_river       1.88%
    sulfur_caves       3.49%        snowy_taiga        1.84%
    windswept_savanna  0.51%        sulfur_caves       0.84%
```

Six biomes each. The 12345 r=18 square adds `ice_spikes` at 0.10% and nothing
else. Twelve distinct biomes in total, out of the **fifty-five** the pinned
jar's own `biome_parameters_overworld.json` lists for the dimension.

So "the overworld at r=16" is, on the flagship seed, a savanna-and-warm-ocean
reading; and on seed 12345 — the seed carrying the only quotable r=16
overworld exactness this project has — it is **78.7% deep frozen water**.
Neither sentence appears beside either figure, and neither could, because a
ledger entry records the window a figure was taken in and there is no field for
what that window is made of.

### 48.2 The decomposition is a null, and it is stronger than the aggregate one

Both windows were already disqualified in aggregate by rule 6. Per biome,
against the N=2 floor taken on the same pair — `worldgen-dump full` at the
order read off the reference, twin-masked, vanilla first, `--bbox 48 48 80 80`:

```text
  seed 20260722          ours   floor(N=2)   ratio    win%   res%
    lukewarm_ocean       2387        6941     0.34   39.35  43.06
    savanna              1714        8105     0.21   42.75  30.92
    sulfur_caves          874       12969     0.07    3.48  15.76
    river                 434         730     0.59    7.44   7.83
    beach                 135         697     0.19    6.48   2.44
    windswept_savanna       0         118     0.00    0.51   0.00
                    -----------------------
    aggregate            5544       29560     0.19

  seed 12345
    frozen_ocean         1834       13896     0.13   78.70  51.62
    sulfur_caves         1213        1698     0.71    0.84  34.14
    snowy_beach           244        1104     0.22    8.31   6.87
    frozen_river          145         360     0.40    1.88   4.08
    snowy_plains           79         472     0.17    8.43   2.22
    snowy_taiga            38         249     0.15    1.84   1.07
                    -----------------------
    aggregate            3553       17779     0.20
```

**Not one biome of either window is above its own floor.** That is a different
and harder statement than the aggregate one. An aggregate under its floor
leaves open the possibility that some sub-window inside it still resolves —
that is exactly what happened in the nether, where `soul_sand_valley` at 14.1x
its floor was hiding inside an r=16 aggregate sitting under its own. Here
there is no such sub-window. The r=16 overworld square is exhausted per biome,
and the next overworld information cannot come from a sharper reading of it.

The masked residuals above, 5,544 and 3,553, are also both far below the 29,490
and 77,067 that `docs/PARITY.md` section 2 still carries — fifteen days of
`main` plus, on the 20260722 window, the dump order now being *read* rather
than assumed. They are recorded here as measurements and are not quotable as
exactness for the reason the table itself gives.

### 48.3 `sulfur_caves` is the one name to carry out

It is the only biome in either window that is over-represented in the residual:
3.49% of the 20260722 square and 15.76% of its residual, 0.84% of the 12345
square and 34.14% of its residual. It is the only biome in either that gets
within 0.71 of its floor. Per block it is this port's weakest overworld biome
by an order of magnitude — 99.976528% and 99.865107% against 99.99x for every
surface biome beside it.

**And it is the only cave biome either window contains.** `dripstone_caves`,
`lush_caves` and `deep_dark` are in neither, at any radius, on any published
seed. `minecraft:large_dripstone` is simultaneously this port's single largest
feature refusal by count — 543, 2049, 1977 and 890 declines in four r=7 squares
scouted outside the published centre. A feature that is refused thousands of
times per window, in a biome family no published window contains, is invisible
to every number in `docs/PARITY.md` by construction.

Vanilla's own reproducibility is also worst there, which is why the ratio is
0.07 rather than something adjudicable: 12,969 of the 29,560 floor — 43.87% of
it — falls in 3.49% of the window. That is the nether's `basalt_deltas` shape
in a smaller biome, and it means `sulfur_caves` will not be adjudicated by
widening N on this square either.

### 48.4 What this changes about choosing a window

A window is chosen by centre and radius, and a biome that no centre lands on is
not measured badly — it is not measured. Forty-three of the overworld's
fifty-five biomes are in that position, including `plains`, `forest`, `desert`,
`jungle`, `swamp`, `taiga`, `badlands`, `deep_dark`, `lush_caves`,
`mangrove_swamp`, `cherry_grove` and `pale_garden`. `ci/ow-biome-scout.sh`
finds squares for them without harvesting anything, and
`ci/ow-biome-coverage.py` reports which few squares cover the most.

### 48.5 CORRECTION — there are three published windows, not two, and the count was 18 of 55 rather than 12

**Seed 31415926's overworld references exist and this section was written as
though they did not.** They are not named `gen-wide-*` like every other
window's; they are `floors-ow31415926r16-{a..h,rr-a,rr-b}` under
`~/rustmc-floors/oracle-work`. Two searches keyed on the other naming family —
one on the presence of a `HARVEST-ORDER`, one on the `gen-wide-` prefix — each
returned nothing and each looked conclusive. The counts above were published on
that basis and are wrong.

The window holds **ten biomes**, more than either of the other two:

```text
  31415926 r=16 c(64,64)
    lukewarm_ocean      61.74%      lush_caves           0.94%
    beach               22.17%      plains               0.73%
    savanna              5.92%      deep_lukewarm_ocean  0.34%
    river                3.77%      forest               0.25%
    sulfur_caves         2.20%      sparse_jungle        0.23%
    jungle               1.72%
```

So the corrected count is **18 of 55 measured and 37 with no figure at all**,
not 12 and 43. `jungle`, `plains`, `forest`, `lush_caves`, `sparse_jungle` and
`deep_lukewarm_ocean` are the six this section called unmeasured and are not.

**Two things survive the correction and one is sharpened.** The six are in the
window `docs/PARITY.md` §2 calls *corroboration only, never quoted on its own*
— its residual of 39,143 sits under its floor of 49,570 — so they appear in a
window carrying no quotable figure, which is a weaker statement than measured
and a different one from unmeasured. And the per-biome decomposition of that
window is the same null as the other two, with a single marginal exception:

```text
  31415926        ours   floor(N=2)   ratio    win%
    lush_caves    5765         5672    1.02    0.94
    jungle        4388         6644    0.66    1.72
    lukewarm_o    3798        21520    0.18   61.75
    sulfur_caves  1400        12310    0.11    2.19
    savanna        800         1314    0.61    5.92
    beach          351         5865    0.06   22.18
    river            4          372    0.01    3.77
```

`lush_caves` at 1.02x is the only biome in any of the three published squares
that clears its own floor, over 0.94% of the window, which is as thin as this
project quotes anything. **The r=16 overworld corpus therefore has one marginal
biome and not zero** — and the sentence in §48.2 is about the 20260722 and
12345 squares, which is what it was measured on.

The 5,765 also corroborates window A's `lush_caves` reading of 4,392 at 1.04x
from a different seed and a different centre entirely: two independent squares,
both landing just above a floor of the same order. A biome that reads 1.02 and
1.04 in two unrelated windows is telling you the instrument is at its limit
there, not that the port is nearly right.

The two exactness figures that window carries, stated with their verdicts
beside them because a bare percentage over a biome is exactly what §48 exists
to stop:

* `lush_caves` — **99.425133%**, 5,765 of 1,002,840, against a floor of 5,672.
  Quotable at 1.02x, and quotable only just.
* `jungle` — **99.760991%**, 4,388 of 1,835,916, against a floor of 6,644.
  🚫 **Not quotable. Not one biome of either window is above its own floor** is
  the general finding and this is an instance of it: the two vanilla runs
  disagree with each other over more of this biome than we disagree with
  either, so the number measures the harvest and not the port. `jungle` is
  under its floor in **both** windows that contain it — 0.66 here and 0.53 in
  the r=7 square at (-480,-1440) — which is why `ci/ow-new-windows.sh` refuses
  to treat porting `minecraft:cocoa` as a validatable change.

## 52. The vanilla draw oracle aborted silently for nine days, and a partial run reads exactly like a small answer (2026-08-18)

`ci/decorate-once.sh … -Ddecorate.draws=<substring>` is the only readable form
of **vanilla's** draw sequence. It prints `DRAW <n> <method>(<bound>) -> <value>`
in the same format `RUSTMC_TRACE_DRAWS` prints, so the two diff line for line.
From 2026-08-09, when `sculk_patch_deep_dark` started generating, it could not
finish any run whose neighbourhood contained a deep dark.

`GenLevel` — **this project's fake `WorldGenLevel`, not vanilla** — threw
`UnsupportedOperationException` on `WorldGenLevel.playSound/6`, and after that
was fixed, on `levelEvent/3`. `SculkSpreader$ChargeCursor.update` calls both.

### Why it survived nine days

The failure has no signature in the place people read.

* The run writes **no JSON**, and `ci/decorate-once.sh` exits 1 for that — so
  the script did report a failure.
* But `-Ddecorate.draws` streams its trace to **stderr as the run goes**, so
  the log file is already on disk and complete-looking: every chunk before the
  sculk patch, in full, and no trace at all of the ones after it.
* A reader with the log open sees a normal log. A five-centre `trees_jungle`
  run reported **three** chunks and was read as *"only three chunks in this
  window have jungle"*. With the harness fixed the same command reports
  **fifteen**.

`docs/cold-start/sculk-deep-dark.md` recorded the jungle's remaining defect as
unresolvable on the ground that *"our own draw count is readable through
`RUSTMC_TRACE_DRAWS` and vanilla's is not"*. Vanilla's was readable the whole
time.

> **A partial run and a small answer are the same printout.** Before believing
> a harness that reported less than you expected, ask whether it finished —
> and if its output has no way to say, that is the defect to fix first.

### The repair, and the repair to the repair

Both methods are `0: return` in `WorldGenRegion` under `javap -p -c -constants`,
so handling them as no-ops is the reference rather than a stub. They were found
one at a time and then **enumerated**: `SculkSpreader`,
`SculkSpreader$ChargeCursor`, `SculkBlock` and `SculkVeinBlock` make exactly
five distinct `LevelAccessor` calls between them — `getBlockState`,
`getFluidState`, `setBlock`, `playSound`, `levelEvent` — and the other three
were already handled, so there is no third gap on that path.

`DecorateGolden` now closes every run with
`DRAWS-END <feature> chunk x,z draws=N`, and `ci/decorate-once.sh` pairs BEGIN
against END and exits 3 on a mismatch, naming the chunk the run died in — which
is the one thing the stack trace does not carry.

**The terminator went in below the `!record` guard first, and that was wrong in
the way this section is about.** Neighbourhood chunks `continue` before that
point and they are exactly the ones a draw run reads, so the pairing check fired
on every healthy run: ten BEGINs and two ENDs. Caught by running it, not by
reading it. Above the guard it is ten and ten.

### What the working oracle then said

Sixteen chunk-runs of `trees_jungle` compared line for line, each aligned to
the 3x3 neighbourhood `DecorateGolden` actually decorates. Two are
byte-identical. In every other, the first divergence is a tree that one side
places and the other does not, **in both directions** — which no constant
per-tree draw deficit can produce, and which withdraws
`docs/PARITY-ROADMAP.md` §67's localisation to "about two draws per mega
jungle tree". The first divergence decomposes with no draw difference in it at
all: identical `count`, identical `in_square`, identical selector floats, and
then vanilla's `OCEAN_FLOOR` reads **87** where ours reads **114**, because a
mega jungle canopy from an earlier chunk stands twenty-seven blocks above that
column in our world and not in vanilla's. `docs/PARITY-ROADMAP.md` §76.

## 53. An identity hash is not a random number, and an order keyed on one is stable exactly when its insertions are (2026-08-19)

This project has twice reasoned from "identity hash order is random per run".
It is not, and the correction changes what two standing measurements mean
without changing either measurement.

Measured by `ci/idhash-determinism.sh`, two probes, five cold JVMs per arm:

* the same allocation sequence produces the **same** identity hashes on every
  run, in a single-threaded arm and in a four-thread arm alike. HotSpot's
  per-thread hash state is seeded from a fixed constant;
* the hash is drawn from that per-thread sequence at the moment `hashCode()`
  is **first called**, not when the object is allocated. Hash the same eight
  objects in a different order and every one of them gets a different number,
  and the values are the same eight values redistributed.

So the identity hash a JVM hands out is the k-th value of a per-thread
deterministic sequence, where k is how many identity hashes that thread has
already drawn. It is a property of the **draw order**, and for a hash
container the draw happens on first insert. Which gives the rule:

> **An identity-hash iteration order is run-stable exactly when the order of
> first-hash calls into that container is run-stable.** It contributes no
> nondeterminism of its own; it only exposes whatever the insertion sequence
> already had.

### What this changes about `broadcastChangedChunks`

`ServerChunkCache.chunkHoldersToBroadcast` is a `ReferenceOpenHashSet` and was
measured at **4.24% self-agreement over four chunks against 1/4! = 4.17%** —
n! near-uniform. That number stands. What it is evidence *of* moves: not that
identity hashing is random, but that **which chunk is first marked dirty
varies between runs**. The nondeterminism is upstream in the chunk pipeline
and the set is the instrument that made it visible.

### And the corollary is the unwelcome half

A container whose insertions are one thread doing the same thing on every boot
has no upstream nondeterminism to expose. Its order is therefore **stable
forever**, vanilla agrees with itself about it in every run, and a check
comparing it would be comparing a permutation drawn from a JVM's PRNG. That is
a harder ceiling than an unseeded draw such as the spawn yaw: an unstable
field announces itself by flapping, and a stable permutation looks matchable
and sits red.

`ci/order-keyed-census.py` holds the three such sites in the pinned jar that
reach the wire. Two are `AttributeMap`'s sync sets; the third is the chunk
broadcast above. `ci/order-selfconsistency.py` measures which of them vanilla
in fact agrees with itself about, and the split is real: over N = 20 distinct
recordings, a mob's `update_attributes` payload is byte-identical in 19 of 19
runs and a **player's** is not, at 4 distinct in 19.

`docs/PARITY-ROADMAP.md` §101 is the finding; `docs/cold-start/entity-id-and-order-keyed.md` is the reference.

## 54. A container's walk order is its whole state machine, not its hash function

Two lanes have now reached a place where matching a JVM container's iteration
order was the parity requirement. §53 covered the case where it cannot be
matched — an identity hash, which belongs to a draw order rather than to the
objects. This is the case where it can, and the trap in doing so.

**The hash function is one input of ten.** For
`it.unimi.dsi.fastutil.ints.Int2ObjectOpenHashMap`, whose walk §103 ported, the
order a key set comes out in is decided by:

| decides the TABLE'S STATE | decides the WALK |
|---|---|
| `mix`, `nextPowerOfTwo`, `arraySize`, `maxFill`, the constructor's `(expected, f)`, the probe step, the growth rule, the shrink rule, the deletion algorithm | the iterator's start and direction |

Nine of the ten build the table and exactly one walks it. So an implementation
can transcribe the hash and the iterator perfectly — the two things a reader
thinks of as "the order" — and still walk a different one, because it built a
different table to walk.

Three of the nine are the ones that actually bite, and all three are invisible
to a corpus of small key sets:

* **the starting size is not the expected size.** `arraySize(16, 0.75)` is
  `nextPowerOfTwo(ceil(21.33)) = 32`. A map that starts at 16 walks a different
  order for every key set from the first one;
* **the growth test and the growth target read the size on opposite sides of an
  increment** — `if (size++ >= maxFill) rehash(arraySize(size + 1, f))`. Taking
  both from one side moves the table size at exactly one boundary, so the walk
  is wrong only for maps of a particular size;
* **removals can shrink the table.** This is the one that breaks the tempting
  summary. "The walk is a pure function of the key set" is true at a fixed
  capacity — linear probing is order-independent, and a shift-back deletion
  preserves that — and false in general, because the capacity is a function of
  the whole insert-and-remove history.

> Two maps holding identical keys walk them in different orders if their
> histories differ. A golden table of key sets cannot see that, whatever its
> size.

**So grade a port on histories, not on sets.** §103's golden is 948 measured
`+k` / `-k` histories rather than 41 key sets, chosen so that some grow past a
table boundary and drain back through the shrink rule. The 41 sets it replaced
would have been passed by an implementation that never shrinks.

And the general form, which is the reason this is filed here rather than in the
roadmap:

> Porting a container's iteration order means porting its state machine. If the
> port cannot be shown to disagree with the original on a history that neither
> the hash nor the iterator can distinguish, the port has not been graded.

One corollary for whoever does this next. Once a container is ours, a check can
no longer *state* our order from a source read the way it could while the
container was the language's — `BTreeMap` walks ascending by contract, and
`IntOpenHashMap` walks fastutil's order by our own code being right. A check
that reads our source and then states our walk asserts the thing it grades. It
has to run it. See §103.5.1.

## 33. §32.3's unexplained ~200 blocks: the isolated arm was not build-sensitive, it was not a function of its inputs at all (2026-08-12)

Branch `worldgen-isolated-drift`, off `main` **d049ef5a** with
`worldgen-generate-once` **dd6f029d** merged in, Threadripper.

§32.3 recorded this and could not explain it: `RUSTMC_DECORATE_ISOLATED=1`
moved ~200 blocks across three builds against code changes that touched only
the other arm, while reporting the arm **byte-identical to itself** on a
sample of two dumps. It registered the observation rather than chasing it,
because 0.5% of a 4.8x effect on a switched-off path is not where the value
was that day.

**The framing was wrong, and the sample size is why.** The arm is not
build-sensitive. It was never a function of its inputs at all: one binary,
one seed, one window, and it produces a different world most times you run
it. Two dumps is the sample size at which a scheduling-dependent defect is
most likely to look clean, and it looked clean.

### 33.1 The cheaper test, taken first

Determinism has a cheaper instrument than parity, and it needs no reference:
the same binary, the same seed, the same window, run twice, must produce
byte-identical output. `ci/isolated-selfrepro.sh` takes it the other way from
§32.3 — N runs at each of several `RUSTMC_WORKERS`, every pair cross-diffed,
no vanilla side and therefore no parity claim to argue about. An ours-vs-ours
diff of one binary is either zero or the arm is not a function of the seed.

**Its floor is 0 and that is by construction, not by measurement**, which is
the one place in this document where that sentence is allowed: both sides are
the same binary given the same seed, the same window and the same decoration
order, over the same 225 of 225 chunks, so there is no second run of vanilla
to disagree with itself and nothing for rule 6 to adjudicate. Every figure
below is therefore a residual over a floor of zero, and one block is a
finding.

Seed 20260722, r=7 at centre (64,64), 225 chunks, 22,118,400 blocks. Nine
runs of each arm — three at `RUSTMC_WORKERS=1`, three at 12, three at the
default — and all 36 pairs diffed.

```text
  arm                  runs   thread counts   distinct worlds   worst pair
  extent (shipped)        9   1 / 12 / auto                 1            0
  isolated                9   1 / 12 / auto                 3          124
```

The shipped arm is **0 on all 36 pairs**, which is the control that says the
noise, surface and carver stages — all of them parallel, all of them running
identically under both arms — are clean, and that what follows belongs to the
isolated decoration driver and to nothing upstream of it.

**And that control is not vacuous, which is the question ORACLE's "a gate a
window cannot exercise proves nothing" asks.** The spider corridors that break
the isolated arm are *in this window* — §33.2 names the two chunks — so the
shared arm ran the same `spider_placed` claim over the same corridors nine
times and came back 0 nine times. Its level barriers hold where the flat queue
does not, measured rather than argued.

The isolated arm's nine runs fall into three classes:

```text
  class   runs                     vs sequential
  S       w1r1  w1r2  w1r3                     0   (the three sequential runs)
  A       w0r1  w12r2 w12r3                    1
  B       w0r2  w12r1                         99
  C       w0r3                               123
```

with A—B 100, A—C 124, B—C 24. **Every one of the six parallel runs differs
from the sequential run**, and the three sequential runs are identical to each
other. `RUSTMC_WORKERS` is a `OnceLock`, so the sequential arm is the same
code path taking the same chunks in decoration rank order; it is deterministic
because nothing races, not because it is a different model.

### 33.2 What moved, and what it names

`region-differ diff --positions` on the A—B pair, 100 blocks: `cobweb`,
`rail`, `oak_fence`, `wall_torch`, `oak_planks`, `deepslate` against
`cave_air`, and on the B—C pair a `spawner` that is present in one run and
absent in the other. Every position sits in a mineshaft's box, around
(1136..1151, −41..−32, 914..927) and (1120..1135, −38..−32, 1008..1021) —
chunks (71,57) and (70,63), two corners of the window.

That is not a lump of drifting terrain. It is one structure's random stream
running one draw out of phase, which is exactly what a mineshaft looks like
when a value is spent that should not have been: cobweb coins, rail coins and
chest coins are all `nextInt` draws in the same corridor pass.

### 33.3 The mechanism, and its own comment names it

`tools/worldgen-assembly/src/generate.rs`, the declaration of `spider_placed`
— written before this branch existed and correct when it was written:

> A `Mutex` for the pool. Unlike the processor-list cache this one really is
> order-dependent — whichever chunk post-processes a spider corridor first
> spends the spawner's draw — **so it is only safe because the two chunks that
> could race for one entry cannot run at the same time: a corridor piece is at
> most 19 blocks long, so it spans at most three chunks in an axis, i.e. two
> apart, and the parallel driver below orders any two chunks within
> `2 * DECORATE_REACH` (>= 2) of each other.**

The safety argument is the shared-canvas pass's **level barriers**. The
isolated arm has none, and that is not an oversight — it is the one thing the
model buys. §32.3's own words: *"the isolated arm's chunks are independent by
construction so its driver is a flat work queue"*, and `IsolatedDecoration`'s
module docs: *"It also makes the pass embarrassingly parallel and retires the
level barriers the shared-canvas pass needed."* Retiring the barriers retired
the premise of the one piece of shared mutable state the pass still had.

`MineShaftCorridor.hasPlacedSpider` is a field on the **piece**, not on the
call — vanilla saves it as `hps` in the piece NBT — and one corridor is
post-processed once per chunk it overlaps. In
`crates/rustmc-worldgen/src/structure/mineshaft.rs`:

```rust
if spider && !*placed_spider {
    let spawner_z = z - 1 + random.next_i32_bound(3);
    if w.inside_box(1, 0, spawner_z) && w.is_interior(1, 0, spawner_z) {
        *placed_spider = true;
        w.place_block("minecraft:spawner", 1, 0, spawner_z);
    }
}
```

The `nextInt(3)` is spent whenever the flag is false. The flag is then set
only if **both** gates pass, and `isInterior` answers false outside the
chunk's clip — so the draw is spent in every chunk the corridor reaches and
the flag is set in the one that actually contains the spawner. So
two chunks sharing one spider corridor, running at the same time, both read
`false`, both spend the draw, and every later value in the losing chunk's
mineshaft pass lands one place early. Which chunk that is, is thread
scheduling.

### 33.4 What was ruled out, and how

Rule 9 — measurements move suspects — so these are eliminations, not
readings.

* **A hash container's iteration order.** Eliminated by measurement first:
  a `HashMap`/`HashSet` iteration would move run to run *and* would move the
  `extent` arm, which walks the same upstream stages; `extent` is 0 on all 36
  pairs. Then by construction: the one `std::collections::HashMap` in the
  decoration driver (`level_of`) is in the **shared-canvas** branch and is
  only ever `get`, and the canvas is `BTreeMap` throughout with a comment
  saying why.
* **`f32`/`f64` reordered by codegen.** Eliminated by the same measurement.
  Codegen cannot differ between two runs of one binary, and the arm moves
  between two runs of one binary. Whatever §32.3 saw across three builds, it
  did not need three builds to see.
* **An uninitialised or reused buffer.** The isolated pass allocates a fresh
  `clone_region` canvas per chunk and a fresh `Vec` write log per chunk; both
  are `Vec`/`BTreeMap`, so there is no uninitialised path to have.
* **A global or `thread_local` carried between tiles.** Enumerated rather than
  assumed: every `thread_local!` in `crates/rustmc-worldgen` is either a
  tracing switch read off the environment, a draw counter, or
  `biome::manager`'s one-cell `fiddles` memo, which is keyed by
  `(seed, parent_x, parent_y, parent_z)` and returns bit-for-bit what
  recomputing returns. And the whole class is bounded from above by the
  compiler: `std::thread::scope`'s closures require the captured shared state
  to be `Sync`, so nothing reachable across the isolated workers can be a
  `Cell` or a `RefCell` at all. That leaves the `Mutex`es, and there are
  three: the processor-list cache (a pure cache, and its comment says so),
  the refusal list (a diagnostic `Vec`), and `spider_placed`.
* **Rayon or a thread-count dependence in the scheduling itself.** There is no
  rayon here; the driver is a hand-written `AtomicUsize` work queue. And a
  thread-count dependence is what the fix below turns into its own control:
  after it, workers 1, 12 and auto agree.

### 33.5 The fix is the model, not a lock

A `Mutex` cannot repair this. The flag is genuinely cross-chunk state, and any
serialisation of it makes a chunk's writes depend on which other chunks ran
first — which is precisely the dependence `RUSTMC_DECORATE_ISOLATED=1` exists
to remove. So the memory is chosen by the decoration model:

* **`SpiderMemory::Pass`** — one memory for the whole pass, `Mutex`ed, the
  shared-canvas arms. Vanilla's carry, kept, because a redraw there is wrong.
* **`SpiderMemory::Chunk`** — one memory per decorating chunk, built inside
  the worker that owns it, no lock needed. Under isolation a chunk decorates
  as if no other chunk exists, and **a flag another chunk set is another
  chunk's write reaching this one by a different name.** It was the only such
  reach left in the pass.

### 33.5b After: nine runs, one world, and the price barely moves

The same nine-run sweep on the fixed binary, same seed, same window, same
three thread counts:

```text
  arm                  runs   thread counts   distinct worlds   worst pair
  extent (shipped)        9   1 / 12 / auto                 1            0
  isolated                9   1 / 12 / auto                 1            0
```

**0 mismatches on all 36 pairs.** That is also what closes §33.4: if anything
else in the pass had been carrying state between chunks, one memory moving to
per-chunk would not have taken the whole spread to zero.

And the price, re-taken on the fixed binary — **VANILLA FIRST**, with
`ci/ref-chunk-extent.py` exit 0 on both references and on all three of our
dumps before any of it was believed:

```text
                       van-rows-c64   van-s1-c62   vs ours(shared)
  ours(shared)              18,779        8,712        —
  ours(extent)              18,729        8,762           50
  ours(isolated)            42,154       42,942       39,865
```

The `shared` and `extent` columns are **§32.3's to the block** — 18,779 /
8,712 and 18,729 / 8,762, and the 50 between them — and that is a weak check
because both columns take the `Pass` branch, which is the old code. The strong
one is a byte comparison: the post-fix `extent` dump against a pre-fix
`extent` dump over the whole window, **0 mismatches of 22,118,400**. The
shipped model did not move, and that is measured rather than argued from the
signature of the change.

The `isolated` column moves **69 blocks** against each reference — 42,085 ->
42,154 and 42,873 -> 42,942 — which is *inside* the ±124 that arm used to
wander on its own between two runs of one binary. Every chunk now spends the
spawner draw its own corridor asks for, so the arm is slightly further from
vanilla again, and it is now the same distance every time. Rule 6 is
untouched: ~42k against **both** references against a reference-pair spread of
10,067.

**The End city window needs no re-take on the isolated arm and one on the
shipped one.** `place_mineshaft_in_chunk` is reached only from the overworld
driver; `generate_end` never constructs a `SpiderMemory`. `ci/end-city-window.sh`
was re-run anyway, because "the code says it cannot have moved" is rule 9's
losing side of the argument.

### 33.6 What this costs §32, and what it does not

**§32.3's `isolated` column was one sample from a distribution.** The three
builds' readings — 42,108 / 42,898, then 42,191 / 42,783, then 42,085 /
42,873 — are three draws from the run-to-run spread this section measures, not
three builds' answers, and no code change between those builds needs to
explain anything. §32.3's own hedge, that nothing quotes the arm to better
than "about 42,000", is what keeps its conclusion intact: the arm's distance
from vanilla is ~42k against **both** references against a reference-pair
spread of 10,067, rule 6 fires, and a ±124-block scheduling wobble does not
touch that. The 4.8x stands; so does the End city window's 99.998861%, whose
168 blocks are all `chorus_plant`/`chorus_flower` and none of them a
mineshaft.

**What does not survive is §32.3's sentence "Both models are byte-identical to
themselves: two dumps of each, same binary, 0 mismatches over 22,118,400
blocks."** For the isolated arm that reading was luck: two dumps land in the
same class about a third of the time. Corrected in place in §32.3 rather than
left to be discovered a second time.

**And the deeper reading is §32.4's, sharpened.** §32.4 argued that
extent-independence and vanilla's neighbour *reads* are not both available.
This is a second, narrower instance of the same trade with a different
mechanism: extent-independence and vanilla's **structure-piece state** are not
both available either. `hasPlacedSpider` is per-piece and spans chunks by
construction; a model in which a chunk's writes are a pure function of the
seed and the chunk's position cannot represent it, and the honest thing is to
say so in the type rather than to lock it and hope.

### 33.7 The instrument, and the next thing it should be pointed at

`ci/isolated-selfrepro.sh <seed> <radius> <cx> <cz> <model> <n> [workers...]`.
It costs no reference, no oracle and no corpus lock, and it answers a question
no parity figure can: whether the number you are about to quote is a property
of the code or of the machine that ran it. **A parity measurement of an arm
that is not self-reproducible is measuring the scheduler**, and this project
has never had a cheap way to notice.

The obvious next target is the one this section could not reach: the same
sweep on the **nether** and **End** drivers, which have their own
`IsolatedDecoration` call sites (`generate_nether`, `generate_end`) and no
mineshaft, so a non-zero there would be a second mechanism rather than this
one. It closes when both come back 0 over nine runs at three thread counts,
or names what moved.

## 34. The 35 `sulfur -> cinnabar` survive a second generation: chunk (77,47) adjudicated against the r=18 twins, floor 0 (2026-08-12)

Base sha **`d049ef5a9c504ce61af44b939c7b19b0d9fe4b65`**, `main` at 2026-08-12
08:08 BST. Recorded first and prominently because this measurement is only
valid against the generator that produced it: `worldgen-decoration-margin` is
live on whether `DECORATION_MARGIN = 2` converges, and if it lands a
behavioural change every figure below is void and must be re-run. **At this sha
that branch points at exactly `d049ef5a` and `DECORATION_MARGIN` is still 2**,
so nothing of its has landed yet.

### The question §30 could not answer about itself

§30 charged chunk (77,47) with **49 blocks, of which 35 are
`sulfur -> cinnabar`**, against the forty-reference seed-12345 r=16 pool, at an
innocent leave-one-out floor of 0. What it could not say is whether those 35
are a port defect or an artefact of **where the references stopped
generating**. Every one of those forty references completes chunks 46..82 and
no further, so (77,47) sits **one ring inside the completed edge** — and a
chunk that close to the edge can differ for no reason but the truncation. The
pool is blind to exactly this: all forty are truncated identically, so their
leave-one-out floor cannot see it. Widening the window is no escape either;
the references do not hold a wider window and `ci/ref-chunk-extent.py` refuses
one.

Only a **second generation at a larger radius** settles it, and it has to be
twinned or ORACLE rule 6 has no precondition. `gen-wide-12345-r18-ow-x1` and
`-x2` are two independent vanilla runs harvested 2026-08-11, identical in every
`HARVEST-ORDER` parameter — `gen_order=rows`, `dump_order=zx`, `seed=12345
radius=18 centre=64,64 dimension=overworld`. They complete chunks **44..84**,
which puts (77,47) **three rings inside** the completed edge instead of one.

### The window is derived, not chosen

`ci/ref-chunk-extent.py --centre 64 64` reports **r=20, window `44 44 84 84`**
on both twins — 1,681 chunks, 165,249,024 blocks — and
`worldgen-dump full 12345 20` writes exactly 44..84. Every side of every diff
below is `minecraft:full` across all 1,681 of them, checked rather than
assumed.

### The floor, derived from the twins themselves and never subtracted

The r=16 adjudication's floor belongs to the r=16 pool and is not carried over.

| what | twins against each other | ours against x1 | ours against x2 |
|---|---|---|---|
| window `44 44 84 84`, blocks | **28,668** of 165,249,024 | 47,237 | 47,851 |
| `sulfur -> cinnabar`, window-wide, ordered pair | **9** | — | 1,062 |
| `sulfur -> anything` (miss marginal) | **298** | — | 1,625 |
| `anything -> cinnabar` (surplus marginal) | **9** | — | 1,130 |
| **chunk (77,47), all blocks** | **0** of 98,304 | — | 51 |

The twins' disagreement with each other over that window is **99.982652%** —
the floor for every figure below, quoted beside each of them and subtracted
from none. **On chunk (77,47) it is zero: 0 mismatches of 98,304, the two runs
reproduce that chunk block for block.** An honest zero rather than an absent
measurement.

Window-wide our charge is **99.971415%** against x1 (47,237 blocks) and 47,851
against x2. That level is not quotable:
**N=2, and the residual is 1.65x its own twin floor** of 28,668. It is
registered in `ci/figures.toml` as `ow-r18-12345-level-n2` with
`quotable = false` and no exactness claim is made from this window.

The row over the whole window is
**1,062 against a binding floor of 298** — the larger of the two marginals,
§18.3 — which is 3.56x, so it clears. It is still not the item, and it is
registered `quotable = false` for a reason that has nothing to do with its
margin:
**rim-dominated — 624 of the row's blocks are in a single ring, and the window itself carries no level**.
**The adjudicable figure is the chunk.**

### The answer: the 35 survive, unchanged

    chunk (77,47), pool = the twins, N=1 leave-one-out
    charge 51,  innocent floor  min 0  median 0  MAX 0

      charge  missFl  surpFl  binding  ratio  vanilla -> ours
          35       0       0        0     inf  minecraft:sulfur -> minecraft:cinnabar   CLEAR
           5       0       0        0     inf  minecraft:diorite -> minecraft:tuff      CLEAR
           3       0       0        0     inf  sulfur_spike[tip,up] -> air              CLEAR
           2       0       0        0     inf  gold_ore -> deepslate_gold_ore
           6       0       0        0     inf  six further sulfur_spike states -> air

**35 against a twin-derived floor of 0**, to the block. The chunk's total charge moves
49 -> 51 and the two new positions are `gold_ore -> deepslate_gold_ore`, which
is the pool falling from N=40 to N=2 and not the radius: the charge is
"ours matches NO reference" and is monotone decreasing in N, so an N=2 reading
is >= an N=40 one for free. The row itself does not move at all.

### The 35 read identically under every pool tried

The invariance is the result, not the single reading. Same chunk, same dump,
every pool this box holds:

| pool | N | chunk charge | `sulfur -> cinnabar` | chunk floor |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| r=18 twins {x1,x2} | 2 | 51 | **35** | 0 |
| r=16 {g,h} | 2 | 51 | **35** | 0 |
| r=16 {d,f} | 2 | 49 | **35** | 34 |
| r=16 {c,d} | 2 | 49 | **35** | 230 |
| r=16 {e,f} | 2 | 81 | **35** | 196 |
| r=16 {c,e} | 2 | 277 | 148 | 0 |
| r=16 {c,d,e,f,g,h} | 6 | 49 | **35** | 0 |
| r=16 pool of forty (§30) | 40 | 49 | **35** | 0 |

**A two-reference floor on this chunk is a coin flip and must not be read as a
law.** Across r=16 pairs it draws from {0, 34, 196, 230}; the twins drew 0. Say
it plainly: under {c,d} and {e,f} the row is *at or under* its own floor at
0.31x and would not be quotable from that pair alone. What makes the 35 safe is
that it reads 35 under all of N=2 twins, N=2 r=16 (four pairs of five), N=6 and
N=40 — one number under eight pools, two generation radii and two independent
harvests. The {c,e} outlier is a property of that pair: `c` and `e` are the two
r=16 references that read 99.766% against everything, and the pair charges 148
one way and 33 `cinnabar -> sulfur` the other.

### Three conditions checked rather than assumed

**1. Our own generation extent, and why (77,47) is outside it.**
`DECORATION_MARGIN` of 2 does not converge (§30), so our dump truncates too and
the adjudicated chunk has to sit clear of it. Ours at r=18 (writes 46..82)
against ours at r=20 (writes 44..84), one tip:

    the adjudicated chunk (77,47)   0 of 98,304
    its whole ring z=47             0 of 3,637,248   (37 chunks)
    the r=18 window 46 46 82 82     2,713 of 134,578,176
      ring x=46                     2,020
      ring x=47                       690
      ring x=48                         0
      ring x=49                         0

Our own contamination reaches **exactly two rings inward** from the outermost
written ring and stops dead. (77,47) is three rings in from our r=20 dump's
edge and moves not one block between r=18 and r=20. Our side is not measuring
its own window.

**2. The tip did not move the generator.** Our r=20 dump at `d049ef5a` is
**byte-identical in every region file** to the one taken at `31e4615f`, and
`git diff 31e4615f d049ef5a -- crates/rustmc-worldgen/ tools/` is empty. The
`decorate-order` digest is `627ff2cc4c6f1245` on both.

**3. r=16 and r=18 are one distribution on this chunk, not two.** If the r=16
truncation were writing (77,47) differently, r=16-against-r=18 would separate
from r=16-against-r=16. It does not:

    r16-c vs x1  99.766032    r16-c vs r16-d  99.766032
    r16-d vs x1  100.000000   r16-e vs r16-f  99.800618
    r16-e vs x1  99.766032    r16-g vs r16-h  100.000000
    r16-f vs x1  99.965413    r16-i vs r16-j  99.965413
    r16-g vs x1  100.000000

and every r=16 reference reads **the same value against x1 as against x2**. The
spread is vanilla's own run-to-run variation, which the twins happen to sit at
the clean end of.

### The row per chunk, and three positions the r=18 twins hand back

Same pool, same window, twin floor 0 on every one:

| chunk | `sulfur -> cinnabar`, r=16 pool 40 (§30) | r=18 twins, N=2 | chunk charge |
|---|---|---|---|
| (77,47) | 35 | **35** | 51 |
| (77,48) | 272 | **272** | 389 |
| (78,48) | 25 | **25** | 40 |
| (79,48) | 8 | 34 | 415 |
| (79,49) | 6 | 4 | 175 |
| (80,50) | 1 | 0 | 0 |
| (78,47) | 3 | 3 | 12 |
| (76,47) | 0 | 0 | 4 |

(79,49) falling 6 -> 4 and (80,50) falling 1 -> 0 **cannot be an N effect** —
the charge only grows as N shrinks — so three of the published 312 are spelled
our way by an r=18 twin and are not charged against this generation. (79,48)
rising 8 -> 34 is the N=40 -> N=2 direction and is expected. The three chunks
the item was named for — (77,47), (77,48), (78,48) — do not move by one block.

### What this closes and what it does not

**Closed:** the 35 are a port defect. Vanilla writes `sulfur` at those 35
positions in forty independent r=16 runs and in both independent r=18 runs, and
we write `cinnabar`. The r=16 reading was not measuring its own window.

**Not closed:** the mechanism. This section adjudicates the charge, it does not
attribute it. §21's finding stands — the row is order-inverted and ours — and
`feature/speleothem_cluster.rs` writing sulfur over the noise stage's cinnabar
remains where the census puts it.

**Not available:** any exactness level from the `44 44 84 84` window. 1.65x on
its own floor, N=2.

## 40. The block-entity hole, measured before anything was built: 173 on the quotable window, a floor of 0..13 in which no content ever disagrees, and a reachable share of 160 (2026-08-16)

`docs/PARITY.md` §6 names the largest hole in worldgen parity in one line:
*"Anything that is not a block state. Block entities are not written, so no
chest contents, no spawner contents, no sign text."* Every published worldgen
exactness figure — the quotable seed-12345 r=16 row, **69,288 of
107,053,056**, included — compares block states and is silent here **by
construction**. A chest whose block state is right and whose contents are
absent scores as a match in `diff`, in `census` and in
`ci/admissible-residual.py` alike.

Nothing measured the size of it. This section is that measurement, taken
before a single generation change, so that every number in it is a property
of vanilla rather than a scorecard for us. The instrument is
`region-differ becensus` / `bediff` / `bereach` and the driver is
`ci/be-census-12345.sh`.

**The window is the one that already carries a quotable exactness figure**, so
the block-entity count and the block-state count can be quoted in the same
sentence: seed 12345, overworld, r=16, centre chunk (64,64), 33x33 = 1,089
chunks, bbox `48 48 80 80`, y unclipped, harvest order `rows` read off each
reference's own `HARVEST-ORDER` stamp. The ensemble is the same sixteen
same-order references c..r behind `ow-r16-12345-floor-n15`.

**It needed no vanilla boot and did not take the corpus lock.** Block entities
are in the chunk NBT the harvest already wrote, and `ChunkColumn` has parsed
them into raw NBT all along. This is `ci/corpus-floor.sh`'s trick applied to a
different tag: the expensive part was paid for when the references were made.

### 40.1 How big the hole is

**173 block entities in 1,089 chunks**, against 107,053,056 block states in
the same window. That ratio is the first thing to say out loud, because it
reframes the item: the hole named in §6 is **six orders of magnitude smaller
in positions than the figure it sits beside**. It is not a rounding error —
it is the whole of chest contents, spawner mobs and vault state, which is
gameplay-visible in a way a swapped granite block is not — but nobody should
expect closing it to move a block-state percentage, and nobody should have
been quoting the block-state percentage as if it covered this.

| id | count | owner breakdown |
|---|---|---|
| `minecraft:potent_sulfur` | 32 | `<feature>` 32 |
| `minecraft:decorated_pot` | 31 | `trial_chambers` 31 |
| `minecraft:chest` | 30 | `<feature>` 21, `trial_chambers` 5, `buried_treasure` 1, `ocean_ruin_cold` 1, `ruined_portal_ocean` 1, `shipwreck` 1 |
| `minecraft:dispenser` | 19 | `trial_chambers` 18, `<feature>` 1 |
| `minecraft:mob_spawner` | 19 | `<feature>` 13, `mineshaft` 6 |
| `minecraft:vault` | 13 | `trial_chambers` 13 |
| `minecraft:barrel` | 10 | `trial_chambers` 10 |
| `minecraft:brushable_block` | 7 | `ocean_ruin_cold` 7 |
| `minecraft:trial_spawner` | 7 | `trial_chambers` 7 |
| `minecraft:hopper` | 5 | `trial_chambers` 5 |

Attribution is to the structure whose **piece bounding box** contains the
position, read out of the reference's own `structure_starts` NBT. The piece
index is built from the whole region directory and only the counted entries
are clipped to the bbox, because a structure can start outside a window and
write into it — and the `shipwreck` row is that rule earning its keep: there
is **no shipwreck start inside these 1,089 chunks**, and its chest is
attributed correctly anyway. A bbox-clipped index would have filed it under
`<feature>` and inflated the decoration column.

### 40.2 The floor, which is not zero and is not what anyone would have guessed

Over **all 120 unordered pairs** of the sixteen references:

| statistic | value |
|---|---|
| positions disagreeing, min | **0** |
| positions disagreeing, median | **5** |
| positions disagreeing, max | **13** |
| mean | 5.36 |
| block entities per reference | 171, 172 or 173 |
| **`id` mismatches, summed over all 120 pairs** | **0** |
| **`payload` mismatches, summed over all 120 pairs** | **0** |

**Every position where two independent vanilla runs both have a block entity,
they have byte-identical NBT — in all 120 pairs, without exception.** The
entire floor is *displacement*: a block entity that exists in one run and not
the other, which is the same threaded decoration-order noise that moves the
block states around it.

That is the most useful thing this census found, and it was not predictable
from the block-state floor. It means **block-entity payload parity is exactly
achievable**: the payload is a deterministic function of the position, so
there is no irreducible noise to hide behind and no floor to subtract from a
payload claim. The only floor is on *presence*, and it is 0..13.

The 0-disagreement pairs matter too, and are the reason the max is quoted
beside the median rather than alone: 12 of the 120 pairs agree completely, so
a single lucky pair would have "proved" a zero floor here exactly as one
lucky pair once did for the End (§5). N=120 is why that cannot happen.

### 40.3 Which types are decided at generation time — from the jar, not from assumption

The question that decides whether this work is cheap or expensive is whether
a container's contents are **resolved** at generation time or saved as an
unresolved reference. Read off the references' own NBT, and confirmed in
`PotentSulfurBlockEntity` / `BlockEntity` bytecode:

* **`chest`, `dispenser`, `decorated_pot`, `brushable_block` save an
  UNRESOLVED reference** — `LootTable:string` plus `LootTableSeed:long`, and
  **no `Items` list at all**. A real sample:
  `{LootTable:"minecraft:chests/simple_dungeon", LootTableSeed:-548012762793186881L, components:{}, keepPacked:0b}`.
  So reproducing a generated chest does **not** mean reproducing a loot roll.
  It means reproducing one string, which the feature already knows, and one
  `nextLong` off a stream whose draw order this repo already models. That is
  a draw-order problem, which is the kind this project is good at, rather than
  a whole loot-table port.
* **`barrel` and `hopper` are the exception and split both ways**: 5 of 10
  barrels carry `Items` *and* a `LootTable`, and all 5 hoppers carry `Items`.
  Those are resolved contents and are genuinely more expensive.
* **`mob_spawner` stores its mob resolved**, as
  `SpawnData:{entity:{id:"minecraft:skeleton"}}` plus eight constant shorts
  and an empty `SpawnPotentials`. `monster_room.rs` already draws that mob
  (its `DRAW 66 nextInt(4)` comment), so the value exists in our code today
  and is simply not written out.
* **`potent_sulfur` stores a constant.** `PotentSulfurBlockEntity.<init>`
  sets `waitingCountdown = -1` (`iconst_m1`) and `saveAdditional` writes
  exactly one key, `countdown`. All 32 in the window are byte-identical
  modulo position: `{components:{}, countdown:-1, keepPacked:0b}`.

### 40.4 The reachable share, and the honest ceiling

**What this one is measured against.** One side is an unmodified vanilla reference and the other is THIS SERVER at main 911c2e04 with no block-entity work in it, which is the point: it measures where our block states already agree well enough to hang a block entity on. It is a ceiling on future work rather than a score for present work, so `ours` is the honest baseline and an `unmodified` reading of it would be a category error.

**160 of 173, 92.486%** — measured positionally, not by comparing counts. At
each of vanilla's 173 block-entity positions, `bereach` asks what block *we*
have there and calls it reachable when the block name matches. Reading the
ceiling off two censuses instead is the trap this repo keeps paying for: two
counts can agree perfectly while every position differs.

| owner | reachable | out of reach |
|---|---|---|
| `minecraft:trial_chambers` | **89** | 0 |
| `<feature>` | 63 | 4 |
| `minecraft:mineshaft` | 6 | 0 |
| `minecraft:ruined_portal_ocean` | 1 | 0 |
| `minecraft:shipwreck` | 1 | 0 |
| `minecraft:ocean_ruin_cold` | 0 | **8** |
| `minecraft:buried_treasure` | 0 | 1 |

**This refutes the expectation the item was written under.** The premise was
that with sixteen of twenty structure sets unbuilt, a large share of chests
and spawners would be unreachable. On this window they are not: trial
chambers are **fully** reachable — all 89 of their block entities sit on
blocks we already place, `decorated_pot` 31/31, `vault` 13/13,
`trial_spawner` 7/7, `dispenser` 19/19, `barrel` 10/10, `hopper` 5/5 — and
the entire structural shortfall is `ocean_ruin_cold`'s 8 and one buried
treasure chest. The `README.md` sentence "sixteen of the twenty structure
sets are still unbuilt **and no block entities are written**" pairs a true
clause with a true clause and invites the inference that the first bounds the
second. On this window it does not.

The 13 out of reach are: 7 `brushable_block` (we place `stone`/`gravel` where
vanilla places `suspicious_gravel`), 4 `potent_sulfur` (2 → `deepslate`, 2 →
`sulfur`) and 2 `chest` (→ `gravel`, `sand`). The four `potent_sulfur` and
two `chest` are ordinary block-state displacement and already counted in the
block-state residual; they are not a second defect.

### 40.5 What this census does not cover

One seed, one window, one dimension. A reachable share is reachable *here*: a
structure set with no start inside these 1,089 chunks contributes to neither
column, which is a fact about the window and not about the structure set. In
particular this window contains no village, no stronghold and no monument, so
it says nothing about their block entities. And 173 is a count on a window
whose block-state denominator is 107,053,056; the two are not a ratio anyone
should quote as a parity percentage.

## 41. The first block entity this server writes: `potent_sulfur`, byte-identical where it lands, and a presence charge that sits inside its own floor (2026-08-16)

§40 measured the hole. This is the first class closed out of it, chosen from
that census rather than by taste: **`potent_sulfur` is the single largest id
on the window at 32**, its payload is a constant, and it needs no loot roll,
no draw and no unbuilt structure.

The implementation is `crates/rustmc-worldgen/src/block_entity.rs`, wired into
both `anvil_bridge` column builders. `PotentSulfurBlockEntity.<init>` sets
`waitingCountdown = -1` and `saveAdditional` writes exactly one key,
`countdown`; `PotentSulfurBlock extends BaseEntityBlock` and its
`newBlockEntity` ignores its `BlockState` argument entirely. All from `javap`
on the pinned jar.

**That last point could only have come from the bytecode.** Every one of the
32 entries in the census window is `potent_sulfur_state=wet`, so the data
could not have told us whether the entity is conditional on the state. Keying
the emitter on the state would have worked perfectly on this window and been
wrong everywhere else.

### 41.1 It is a post-pass over finished block states, and that is exact

Vanilla does not write a block entity from the feature that places the block.
`LevelChunk.setBlockState` creates one for any `EntityBlock` state it stores
and drops it when the position is overwritten, so at save time the list is a
function of the **final** block states and nothing else. Deriving it from the
finished canvas is therefore the same function evaluated at the same point,
not an approximation.

It is also the only construction that cannot perturb generation: the canvas is
already final when it runs, so no draw is taken and no block state can move.

**Measured, not assumed.** Over six independent vanilla references the count
of `potent_sulfur` blocks equals the count of `minecraft:potent_sulfur` block
entities in every one — 32/32, 32/32, 32/32, 32/32, 32/32, 30/30. Our output
holds the same invariant: 31 blocks, 31 block entities.

### 41.2 The two figures, and why only one of them resolves

Same window, same ensemble, `region-differ bediff --match potent_sulfur`,
vanilla first.

| | ours vs vanilla | innocent-vanilla floor, n=120 pairs |
|---|---|---|
| `payload` mismatches | **0** | **0** (min 0, max 0) |
| `id` mismatches | **0** | **0** (min 0, max 0) |
| missing + spurious | **7** (4 + 3) | 0..12, median 4, mean 4.71 |
| co-located and exact | **28 of 28** | — |

**What each side of these is.** The payload row: One side is an unmodified vanilla reference and the other is this server with the potent_sulfur emitter in it. The FLOOR beside it is the unmodified-against-unmodified one and is what makes the reading mean something: ow-r16-12345-be-floor records payload mismatches of exactly 0 across all 120 innocent pairs, so the content comparison has no noise to hide in and a single wrong byte would show. The presence row: Our dump against one unmodified vanilla reference, with the floor taken unmodified-against-unmodified over the same ensemble.

**The payload figure resolves and the presence figure does not, and the
census predicted exactly that.** §40.2 found `payload` and `id` mismatches to
be zero in all 120 innocent pairs, so the floor on content is not merely small
— it is **exactly zero**, and a single wrong byte would show. Against that
floor, 28 of 28 co-located entries byte-identical is a real result.

**The presence charge of 7 is below floor and is not quotable as a
level.** A per-class floor of 0..12 at median 4 covers it; rule 6 is not
satisfied and this is stated rather than presented as 80% of something. What
the 7 actually is: the same decoration-order displacement that moves the
`potent_sulfur` *blocks*, which §40.4 already counted in the block-state
residual as 4 out-of-reach positions. It is not a second defect and closing it
means moving blocks, not block entities.

**Read the per-class floor rather than §40.2's aggregate.** The aggregate
block-entity floor is 0..13 over all ten types and `potent_sulfur` alone is
0..12 of it — this one class is nearly the whole of that window's
block-entity noise, which is why a charge on it had to be scored against its
own floor. This is `docs/DETERMINISM.md` §18's lesson in a new place: an
aggregate floor and a named floor are different quantities.

### 41.3 Block-state parity did not move, proved directly

The requirement was that every published block-state figure stay
byte-identical. The strongest available check is the direct one:

    region-differ diff <ours BEFORE> <ours AFTER> --bbox 48 48 80 80
    DIFF chunks=1089  blocks=107053056  mismatches=0  biomes=1672704/0
    EXACTNESS 100.000000%

Zero mismatches over the full 107,053,056-block window, biomes included. The
vanilla-facing diff is unchanged too: **12,707 mismatches before and 12,707
after** against reference `c`, with an identical confusion table and identical
worst-chunk list — the two logs differ only in the directory name printed in
their header.

`ci/t2-regiondiff.sh` is green at **100.000000%** on its fixture pair. Note
what it can and cannot say here: it compares two *vanilla* directories, so it
exercises the Anvil parser and the differ and is structurally incapable of
being moved by a change to our generator. It is reported because it was asked
for, not because it adjudicates this.

**One thing found while running it, recorded because it will mislead the next
person.** Its header asserts *"If vanilla-vs-vanilla isn't 100%, the
parser/differ/gen-harness is broken"*, and that premise is false. Regenerating
the pair from scratch — seed 20260722, r=2, the same 3x3 comparison — gives
**17 mismatches, 99.998079%**, in `sulfur_spike` and `air` — a
vanilla-against-vanilla floor reading, registered as
`t2-regiondiff-fresh-pair-floor` and as `regiondiff-reported-single-harvest`,
both of them **not a level**: seventeen blocks is one draw from the 0..536
distribution this window's floor spans, so the percentage beside it is a
property of that draw and may not be quoted as an exactness. A floor is quoted
beside a charge and never subtracted from one. The frozen fixture
on disk reads 100% and a fresh harvest of the identical window does not, so
the script's demand for bit-exactness holds only for the one pair it already
has. That is vanilla's own decoration-order noise at r=2, consistent with
every other floor in this document, and it is not evidence about the parser.
The script gates nothing today — it is in neither `ci/t0.sh` nor `ci/t1.sh`
nor `ci/handoff.sh`; `oracle-work/t2-regiondiff/` survives as the fixture for
`real_vanilla_chunks_survive_a_rewrite`. Left as found rather than adjusted,
because changing a threshold on a shared artefact is not this branch's item.

### 41.4 What is deliberately not written, and why absence is a list

`block_entity.rs` emits `potent_sulfur` and nothing else. The other nine types
on the census are absent because they are **not yet reproduced**, never
because they need none — and the distinction matters, because a reader who
takes it the other way concludes the chests in a generated world are correct,
which they are not: they have no contents at all.

* `chest`, `dispenser`, `decorated_pot`, `brushable_block` — need the loot
  table id the feature already knows plus one `nextLong` at the right point.
  A draw taken at the wrong point is a *wrong* payload rather than a missing
  one, and against a payload floor of exactly zero a wrong one is fully
  visible. Nothing ships until the draw is pinned.
* `mob_spawner` — 19 positions, all 19 reachable, and
  `feature/monster_room.rs` **already draws the mob** it stores. Eight
  constant shorts and an empty `SpawnPotentials` beside it. This is the next
  item and it is the cheapest remaining one.
* `barrel`, `hopper` — resolved `Items`. Genuinely expensive.
* `vault`, `trial_spawner` — `config`/`server_data`/`shared_data` compounds
  not yet read out of the jar.

### 41.5 The ordering caveat, stated rather than discovered later

Vanilla saves a chunk's `block_entities` in its block-entity **map** iteration
order, which is a hash order: the nineteen entries in chunk (66,79) of
reference `c` come in an order that is neither by y, nor by position, nor by
type. We emit in a deterministic scan order and **do not claim to match it**.

That is sound for every use there is today, because `bediff` keys entries by
position and never reads list order — which is why every figure above is
positional. It is **not** sound for a byte-identical region file, and anyone
who needs one needs that map's bucket layout first.

## 42. The block-entity side channel: what it must carry, and why the nine remaining types are one item rather than nine (2026-08-17)

§40 counted the block entities and §41 landed the one class that is a
constant. This is the measurement that decides how the other nine are built:
**for each class, is the saved payload a function of the finished block state,
or was it written by whatever placed the block?** The first kind can be
derived by the post-pass emitter in `crates/rustmc-worldgen/src/block_entity.rs`.
The second kind cannot, by construction, and needs a generation-time side
channel.

Instrument: `region-differ becensus --fields` and `--raw` over
`gen-wide-12345-r16-ow-c`, seed 12345, overworld, r=16, centre chunk (64,64),
`--bbox 48 48 80 80`, 1,089 chunks, 173 block entities; plus `javap -p -c
-constants` on the pinned `server-26.2.jar`. **No vanilla boot, no dump of
ours, no corpus lock** — this reads references that already exist, in seconds,
on a box that was at load 19.6 running a gate throughout.

### 42.1 The payload of every class, and where each value comes from

| id | n | payload beyond `id`/`components`/`keepPacked`/`x`/`y`/`z` | written by |
|---|---|---|---|
| `potent_sulfur` | 32 | `countdown:-1` | constant — **landed, §41** |
| `decorated_pot` | 31 | `LootTable`, `LootTableSeed`, `sherds` on 8 of 31 | placer |
| `chest` | 30 | `LootTable`, `LootTableSeed` | placer |
| `dispenser` | 19 | `LootTable`, `LootTableSeed` | placer |
| `mob_spawner` | 19 | `SpawnData.entity.id` + 8 constant shorts + empty `SpawnPotentials` | placer (a draw) |
| `vault` | 13 | `config.key_item`, `config.loot_table` on 4 of 13, empty `server_data`/`shared_data` | placer |
| `barrel` | 10 | 5 × `Items:[]`; 5 × `LootTable`+`LootTableSeed` | placer |
| `brushable_block` | 7 | `LootTable`, `LootTableSeed` | placer |
| `trial_spawner` | 7 | `normal_config`, `ominous_config` — 3 distinct pairs | placer |
| `hopper` | 5 | `Items:[]`, `TransferCooldown:0` | placer |

**So one of the ten is derivable and it is the one already done.** The other
141 positions are all placer-written. That is the finding, and it is a
statement about the emitter's signature rather than about any class: a post-pass
over the finished canvas can read the final block states and nothing else.

### 42.2 `LootTableSeed` is a draw, so the loot classes are not cheaper than the spawner

`RandomizableContainer.setBlockEntityLootTable(BlockGetter, RandomSource,
BlockPos, ResourceKey)` calls `RandomSource.nextLong()` and passes the result
to `setLootTable(key, seed)`. The seed is therefore taken from the feature's
own stream at placement time and is no more recoverable from
`("minecraft:chest", x, y, z)` than a spawner's mob is. `block_entity.rs`'s
header called these "tractable, and strictly a draw-order problem"; that is
true of *getting the value right* and false of *getting it at all* without a
channel, and it is corrected there.

### 42.3 Two refutations, both of figures published in §40

* **No block entity in this window carries resolved contents.** §40.3 says
  "5 of 10 barrels carry `Items` *and* a `LootTable`, and all 5 hoppers carry
  `Items`. Those are resolved contents and are genuinely more expensive."
  Read raw, the two barrel groups are **disjoint** — 5 are `{Items:[]}` with no
  loot table, 5 are `{LootTable,LootTableSeed}` with no `Items` — and all five
  hoppers are `{Items:[],TransferCooldown:0}`. Every `Items` list in the window
  is **empty**. Nothing here is a resolved-contents port, and the class that
  looked most expensive is among the cheapest once the channel exists.
* **`vault` is NOT a function of the block state, and the data alone says it
  is.** All 13 split 4/9 between `ominous=true` and `ominous=false`, and the
  payloads split 4/9 between the ominous config
  (`key_item:{id:"minecraft:ominous_trial_key"}` plus
  `loot_table:"minecraft:chests/trial_chambers/reward_ominous"`) and the plain
  one (`key_item:{id:"minecraft:trial_key"}`, no `loot_table`). A perfect
  correlation over 13 positions. **The bytecode refutes it**:
  `VaultBlockEntity.<init>` sets `config` to `VaultConfig.DEFAULT`
  unconditionally — `getstatic VaultConfig.DEFAULT` straight into `putfield
  config` — and never reads `VaultBlock.OMINOUS`. The correlation is the
  trial-chambers template placing the ominous state and the ominous config
  together, not the state deriving the config. Keying the emitter on
  `ominous=` would have passed every test this window can run and been wrong
  the first time a template placed the pair apart.

  This is the same trap `block_entity.rs`'s header already documents from the
  other side: `PotentSulfurBlock.newBlockEntity` **ignores** its `BlockState`,
  so keying on the state there would invent a condition vanilla does not have.
  Both readings came from the bytecode and neither could have come from data.

### 42.4 What the channel has to carry

Per position, one of:

* **`Loot { table: string, seed: i64 }`** — chest 30, dispenser 19,
  decorated_pot 31, brushable_block 7, barrel 5 = **92 positions**;
* **`Spawner { mob: string }`** — 19. One `nextInt(4)` into
  `{SKELETON, ZOMBIE, ZOMBIE, SPIDER}` for the 13 dungeon spawners, already
  drawn and discarded in `feature/monster_room.rs`; the 6 mineshaft ones come
  from their own piece;
* **`TrialSpawner { normal: string, ominous: string }`** — 7, three distinct
  pairs on this window (`breeze/normal` ×2, `ranged/skeleton` ×1,
  `slow_ranged/skeleton` ×4);
* **`Vault { key_item: string, loot_table: Option<string> }`** — 13;
* **`Sherds { [string; 4] }`** — the 8 decorated pots that carry them, three
  distinct patterns, alongside their `Loot`;
* **`Bare`** — barrel 5, hopper 5. Not "no entry": an explicit record that the
  placer set no loot table, which is what makes `{Items:[]}` safe to emit.
  Without it the emitter cannot tell a bare barrel from a loot barrel, since
  both are `minecraft:barrel` in every block state.

### 42.5 What it costs, read off the tree rather than estimated

* **Consumer: two call sites.** `block_entity::from_sections` is called from
  `crates/rustmc-worldgen/src/anvil_bridge.rs::to_chunk_column` (`:99`) and `::column_from_canvas` (`:407`) and nowhere else.
* **Producer: a write-only method on the world traits.** Features take
  `world: &mut W` over a per-feature trait (`MonsterRoomWorld` and its
  siblings), all of which the canvas implements, so the channel is a field on
