  the canvas and a `record_block_entity(pos, payload)` on the traits. **No
  signature threading.** Ten files under `crates/rustmc-worldgen/src/` place a
  block that carries an entity: `feature/monster_room.rs`, `feature/nether.rs`,
  `structure/{mineshaft,ocean_ruin,ruined_portal,template,processor,place}.rs`,
  `stage/decorate.rs`.
* **Invalidation is not optional and is cheap.** `LevelChunk.setBlockState`
  drops a block entity when its position is overwritten, which the post-pass
  gets for free by reading only final states. A position-keyed channel must
  reproduce it: at emit, intersect the channel with the finished canvas and
  drop any payload whose position no longer holds a block of that class.
* **The property that must not be lost**, and the gate for it. The emitter
  runs after generation precisely so it cannot perturb it. A channel is a
  write path out of generation, and it stays sound only while there is no read
  path back in: nothing in a feature may branch on what the channel holds.
  That is checkable rather than assertable — generate the window twice, once
  recording and once not, and require the region files to be **byte-identical
  in their block states**. The same output-identical discipline the perf track
  uses, and the reason to build the channel with the gate rather than after it.
* **A position that needs a payload and has none must emit nothing and be
  counted.** Absence already means "not yet reproduced" in this file; a wrong
  payload would be a new and worse thing, and the count is the falsifier.

## 43. The side channel §42 specified, built — the identity gate holds, three classes land, and the other 101 positions turn out to be waiting on a draw nobody spends (2026-08-17)

§42 measured that the nine remaining block-entity classes are **one
architectural item and not nine ports**, and wrote the channel's spec, payload
and cost. This is that item built, gated, and scored against the reference. It
also moves the ceiling: the thing standing between this server and the other
101 positions is not nine ports and is not the channel. It is a
`RandomSource` that four of five call sites do not thread.

### 43.1 What the channel is, in four pieces

| piece | where | what it is |
|---|---|---|
| the record | `block_entity::Record` | the payload keys a placer had, plus the one value it *drew* — the loot seed — kept apart from them |
| the store | `Canvas::block_entities` | `Option<Records>`, chunk-keyed, insertion-ordered; `None` is not recording and `Some(empty)` is recording nothing |
| the producer | `block_entity::BlockEntityRecorder` | one required method, one direction, no reader |
| the consumer | `block_entity::from_sections_recorded` | called from `crates/rustmc-worldgen/src/anvil_bridge.rs::column_from_canvas` (`:407`), which already held the canvas |

**§42.4's per-class payload vocabulary turned out to be unnecessary, and that
is the largest simplification this item found.** That section listed six record
kinds — `Loot`, `Spawner`, `TrialSpawner`, `Vault`, `Sherds`, `Bare` — as the
shapes the channel must carry. Read out of vanilla's own trial-chambers
templates with the pinned jar's structure NBT, the stored tags *are* the
payloads the references save:

| template tag, distinct shapes over `data/minecraft/structure/trial_chambers` | n |
|---|---|
| `minecraft:trial_spawner` `{normal_config, ominous_config}` | 14 |
| `minecraft:chest` `{LootTable}` | 12 |
| `minecraft:dispenser` `{LootTable}` | 6 |
| `minecraft:barrel` `{LootTable}` | 3 |
| `minecraft:decorated_pot` `{LootTable, sherds}` | 3 |
| `minecraft:vault` `{config:{key_item:{count,id}, loot_table?}}` | 2 |
| `minecraft:dispenser` `{Items:[{count,Slot,id}]}` | 1 |
| `minecraft:barrel` `{Items:[]}`; `minecraft:hopper` `{Items:[], TransferCooldown}` | 1 each |
| `minecraft:decorated_pot` `{LootTable}`; `{item:{count,id}, sherds}` | 1 each |

So `StructureTemplate.placeInWorld` hands that tag to
`BlockEntity.loadWithComponents` and the save writes it back, and **carrying a
payload needs no vocabulary at all — it needs a map.** What is per class is
only the completion the save adds on top, which is `block_entity::complete`
and which has exactly two arms today: `mob_spawner`'s seven counts and
`SpawnPotentials`, and `vault`'s two empty maps. A `Bare` variant is not needed
either: a bare barrel's template tag is `{Items:[]}`, which is a payload rather
than an absence, and it is what makes `{Items:[]}` safe to emit.

### 43.2 The gate §42.5 named, and the half of the condition it cannot see

`ci/be-channel-identical.sh`, seed 12345, centre chunk (61,50), r=4, 81 chunks,
`RUSTMC_WORKERS=1`, one binary run twice:

```
positive control: mob_spawner  plain=0  recording=1
block-state mismatches between the two arms: 0
GREEN
```

**The gate holds.** Recording moves no block state.

Two things about it are worth more than the zero.

**Its first run reported VACUOUS, not GREEN.** The differ was pointed one level
above the `region/` directory the dump writes into, so it saw `chunks=0` and
every count was zero — and *two arms that both wrote nothing agree perfectly*.
The positive control is checked before the comparison for exactly that reason,
so the gate refused itself rather than reporting the false green that this
repo's central defect class is made of.

**And the stated condition is only half of the real one.** On/off identity
cannot see a producer that spends a draw to build its payload, because the draw
is spent in *both* arms: the two worlds move together and agree perfectly while
both are wrong. `ci/be-channel-falsify.sh` injects that defect and **asserts
the identity gate stays green on it**, then shows what does catch it — the
draw-count test in `feature::monster_room`, which compares the feature's draws
against an independent replay of the same stream and runs in T0. The pair is
the finding: an agent holding only §42.5 would take on/off identity for the
whole condition, and it is half.

### 43.3 What landed, scored against the reference, vanilla first

`region-differ bediff oracle-work/gen-wide-12345-r16-ow-c <ours> --bbox 48 48
80 80`, our dump taken with `RUSTMC_DECORATE_ORDER=zx` to match the
reference's own `HARVEST-ORDER` stamp (ORACLE rule 29):

```
BEDIFF chunks=1089 vanilla_be=173 ours_be=71 union=176
  missing=105 spurious=3 id=0 payload=0 exact=68
```

**The union-based exactness that line also prints is deliberately not quoted
here, and it is not registered.** Its denominator mixes two different things —
105 of the 176 are positions where a class is not ported at all — so it is a
progress bar and not a parity figure, and this file does not publish progress
bars. The two figures that mean something are separable and are both stated:
**68 of 68 positions where both have a block entity, key for key**, and a
presence count of 71 against vanilla's 173.

The payload figure is registered with baseline `ours`, not `unmodified`:
Vanilla on one side, THIS SERVER with the channel recording on the other.
It scores the payloads this server writes rather than something vanilla did on
its own, so an `unmodified` reading of it would be a category error.

| class | source of the payload | positions | verdict |
|---|---|---|---|
| `potent_sulfur` | the finished block state — §41, no channel | 32 vanilla, 31 ours | 28 exact, 4 missing, 3 spurious: the presence floor |
| `mob_spawner`, dungeon | the `nextInt(4)` `monster_room` already drew | 13 | exact |
| `mob_spawner`, mineshaft | the constant `cave_spider` | 6 | exact |
| `chest`, `<feature>` | `SIMPLE_DUNGEON` + the `nextLong` already drawn | 21 | exact |

**`id=0` across the whole window**, which is the type-id table doing its job:
the block `minecraft:spawner` saves as `minecraft:mob_spawner` and both
suspicious blocks as `minecraft:brushable_block`, read off `BlockEntityTypes`'
`register(BlockEntityTypeIds.X, ..., validBlocks)` calls in the pinned jar.
Keying the emitter on the block name would have written two type ids that do
not exist in the registry, and every one of those positions would have scored
as an id mismatch rather than as a match.

**The mineshaft spawner is the case that proves the channel is load-bearing
even for a constant.** A mineshaft's spawner and a dungeon's are the same
block, `minecraft:spawner`, and they save different mobs. No post-pass over
finished block states can tell them apart. Corroborated positionally rather
than argued: of the nineteen `mob_spawner` entries in the reference, exactly
the six the census attributes to `minecraft:mineshaft` carry `cave_spider`, and
the thirteen `<feature>` ones split **7 zombie, 5 skeleton, 1 spider** — which
is `MOBS = {SKELETON, ZOMBIE, ZOMBIE, SPIDER}` with its duplicate, and not the
three-name list a reader would write from the distinct names.

The 4 missing and 3 spurious `potent_sulfur` are **not** this item's: §40's
floor is 0..13 positions of pure displacement at median 5, and every position
where two vanilla runs both have a block entity has byte-identical NBT. A
presence charge of 7 sits inside that floor and is quoted beside it, never
subtracted from anything.

### 43.4 The wrong type that scored nineteen positions, and the instrument that hid it

The first `bediff` of this work came back `payload=19`, on all nineteen
`mob_spawner` positions, on exactly seven keys — `Delay`, `MinSpawnDelay`,
`MaxSpawnDelay`, `SpawnCount`, `MaxNearbyEntities`, `RequiredPlayerRange`,
`SpawnRange` — with `SpawnData` and `SpawnPotentials` **matching**. So every
mob was right and every count was the wrong NBT type: written `TAG_Int`, and
`BaseSpawner.save` writes all seven with `ValueOutput.putShort`, seven
`invokeinterface putShort:(Ljava/lang/String;S)V` in a row against
`storeNullable` for `SpawnData` and `store` for `SpawnPotentials`.

**How the wrong type got in is the part worth keeping.** It was read off a JSON
rendering of the reference's own NBT — and a JSON rendering prints a short and
an int identically. The value was measured, from the right reference, at the
right positions, and the instrument had already thrown the answer away. The
bytecode was the only source that could distinguish them.

This is the same class as `0 of 539 stacks opaque` and the rest, one door
further along: not a check that cannot fail, but a **reading taken through a
projection that cannot represent the thing being read**. Ask of any value
copied off a rendering what the rendering flattens. `region-differ bediff`
compares typed NBT and is the instrument that caught it; the unit tests in
`block_entity.rs` all passed with the wrong type, because they compared the
emitter against the same flattened reading.

### 43.5 The other 101 positions are waiting on a draw, not on a class

Every remaining position on this window is a container whose template supplies
a `LootTable` and whose placer never spends the `nextLong` the save writes
beside it:

| owner | positions | why the seed is absent |
|---|---|---|
| `minecraft:trial_chambers` | 89 | `place_piece(..., random: None)` — the jigsaw driver threads no piece stream |
| `minecraft:ocean_ruin_cold` | 8 | `handleDataMarker`'s draw is deliberately not modelled, and the suspicious-sand rule's positional delegate draw is not either |
| `minecraft:shipwreck` | 1 | `random: None` |
| `minecraft:buried_treasure` | 1 | `random: None` |
| `minecraft:ruined_portal_ocean` | 1 | **threads its stream** — this is the one caller that does |

`from_sections_recorded` refuses a `LootTable` with no seed and counts it as
`EmitStats::incomplete`, distinct from `orphaned`. The two absences have
opposite fixes and a single counter would have hidden that: an orphan is the
channel working, an incomplete is a draw somebody has to thread.

**And threading it is a block-state change, which is why it is not a line in
this item.** `place_piece`'s own header has said so since the ruined portal
landed: the seed draw is the only call on the piece's stream, so spending it
moves every later draw in that piece — `RuinedPortalPiece.postProcess` is the
worked example, where `spreadNetherrack`'s `nextInt(max(1, 8 - avgSpan/2))` is
the very next draw and sets the apron's radius. Vanilla enters that pass at
draw 1; entering at draw 0 spreads a ring too far. So the item that unlocks
101 of 173 block-entity positions is a **draw-order** item with a block-state
measurement of its own, and its payoff is now quantified: it is worth 89
trial-chambers positions the moment the jigsaw driver threads a stream.

### 43.6 Two corrections to §42

* **§42.4's six payload kinds are one map.** Not a refutation of the
  measurement, which stands per class — a simplification of the design it
  proposed, from the template side of the same question. See 43.1.
* **§42.3's "every `Items` list in the window is empty" is true of the WINDOW
  and not of the templates.** `trial_chambers/chamber/eruption/center_1.nbt`
  carries a dispenser whose template tag is
  `{Items:[{count:1, Slot:4, id:"minecraft:water_bucket"}]}`. The window's
  emptiness is a fact about which pieces this window happens to contain, not a
  property of trial chambers, and a channel that could only carry empty lists
  would be wrong on the first window that holds that piece. The map carries it
  either way; the claim is what needed narrowing.

### 43.7 The falsifier's measured verdict, and what it cost to make it honest

Run for real, `ci/be-channel-falsify.sh` reports:

```
=== injection 2 — the producer spends an extra draw ===
  caught: monster_room's draw tests went red, as they must
=== injection 2 against the identity gate (expected: green, i.e. blind) ===
  confirmed: the identity gate is green on a defect it cannot see.
=== injection 1 — the channel writes into the world ===
  caught by the comparison: block-state mismatches between the two arms: 6
                            REFUSED  recording changed generation: 6 block state(s) moved.
=== control — the unpatched tree ===
  the clean tree is green, so the reds above are the injections
GREEN
```

**43.2's claim that the stated gate is half the condition is now measured
rather than argued.** The identity gate is *green* on a producer that spends a
draw, and it printed so itself. On the defect it does own it is not subtle:
six block states move in an 81-chunk window, and the refusal names the count.

The control arm of that run is also the gate's own re-confirmation on the
rebased tree — `git archive HEAD` of this branch's tip onto main `bba29ee2`,
built and dumped twice, block states identical.

Three things had to be fixed before that verdict meant anything, and every one
was surfaced by the clean-tree control at the bottom of the script rather than
by inspection:

* **A shared `CARGO_TARGET_DIR` put the binaries where the gate does not look.**
  The gate reads `$ROOT/target/release/...`; it refused for a missing binary,
  and that refusal is indistinguishable from the gate catching an injection.
  Every verdict in the run was unattributable and the control said so.
* **`ln -sfn` put the input symlink INSIDE a directory `tar` had just made.**
  `data/` holds one tracked file, so `git archive HEAD | tar -x` creates a real
  `data` directory, and `ln -sfn /a/b /existing/dir` then writes `dir/b`. The
  scratch tree had `data/data` and no `data/vanilla`.
* **A two-week-stale `block_props.json`** — seeded from the canonical checkout
  rather than from `~/rustmc/data/vanilla`, with **zero** occurrences of
  `randomlyTicking` against 32,367 in a regenerated one. `worldgen-dump`
  refused and named both the column and the script to regenerate with, which is
  a check working: the alternative is silently treating every state as
  non-random-ticking.

And one defect in the *first* green run, which is why this section exists rather
than a line saying the falsifier passed: **injection 1 was being caught for the
wrong reason.** It wrote air at the recorded position, destroying the block it
had just recorded, so the recording arm's census came back empty and the gate
refused as VACUOUS — via its positive control, not via the block-state
comparison the case exists to exercise. It is now `y + 1`, and the script
requires the refusal to be `recording changed generation` and refuses if the
gate goes red some other way. **A guard that catches for a reason other than the
one it claims is a guard nobody can reason about**, and the empty `caught:` line
it printed — a report that something was caught with nothing shown — is what
gave it away.

## 44. The jigsaw driver's piece stream: what it is, what it costs in block states, and why that answer is not the one the item was written expecting (2026-08-17)

§43 closed with a next item and a prediction. The item: `place_piece` is called
with `random: None` at four of its five call sites, so the `nextLong` that
`RandomizableContainer.setBlockEntityLootTable` spends is never spent, and 89 of
the census window's 101 unreproduced block entities are trial-chamber containers
waiting on exactly that draw. The prediction: **"Expect the residual to MOVE;
the question the measurement answers is whether it moves towards vanilla."**

The prediction rests on a premise, and the premise is refutable from the pinned
jar without generating anything. This section reads it, and then measures the
thing the reading predicts.

### 44.1 There are two streams and the item is about the second one

Read out of `server-26.2.jar` with `javap -p -c -constants`. The jigsaw
machinery touches randomness at two entirely separate points, on two different
`RandomSource` objects, and conflating them is the whole difficulty of the item:

* **The LAYOUT stream.** `Structure.GenerationContext`'s `WorldgenRandom`,
  `setLargeFeatureSeed(seed, chunkX, chunkZ)`. `JigsawPlacement.addPieces` and
  `JigsawPlacement$Placer.tryPlacingChildren` spend it on
  `getShuffledJigsawBlocks`, the weighted element pick, the rotation and
  `getRandomNamedJigsaw`. It decides **which pieces exist and where**, and it
  is finished before a block is written. This port already models it; it is not
  what `place_piece`'s `random` parameter is.
* **The WRITE stream, which is the item.** `ChunkGenerator.applyBiomeDecoration`
  builds one `WorldgenRandom` (offsets 90..104), calls
  `setDecorationSeed(seed, minBlockX, minBlockZ)` (offset 127), and then, in the
  structures loop, `setFeatureSeed(populationSeed, structureIndex, step)` at
  **offset 293** — once per structure, not per start and not per piece. That one
  instance is handed to `startsForStructure(sectionPos, structure).forEach(..)`,
  and `StructureStart.placeInChunk` passes it to `postProcess` on every piece
  whose box intersects the writable area, in `pieceContainer.pieces()` order
  (offset 124), and then to `Structure.afterPlace` (offset 147).

`PoolElementStructurePiece.postProcess` forwards it to `place`, which forwards it
to `StructurePoolElement.place`, which for a `SinglePoolElement` is
`StructureTemplate.placeInWorld(level, pos, pos2, settings, random, flags)`.

### 44.2 Inside `placeInWorld` that stream has exactly one consumer, and the tag is drawn at PLACEMENT

This is the load-bearing reading, and it is a negative one, so it is stated as a
search rather than as a claim. Over the whole of `placeInWorld` — offsets 0
through the end of the method — the only instructions that touch the passed
`RandomSource` are:

```
   476: instanceof    #454   // class RandomizableContainer
   481: ifeq          502
   484: aload 21             // StructureBlockInfo
   486: getfield      #193   // .nbt:LCompoundTag;
   489: ldc_w         #456   // String LootTableSeed
   492: aload 5              // the RandomSource
   494: invokeinterface #458 // RandomSource.nextLong:()J
   499: invokevirtual #464   // CompoundTag.putLong:(Ljava/lang/String;J)V
   528: invokevirtual #477   // BlockEntity.loadWithComponents:(LValueInput;)V
```

One `nextLong` per placed `RandomizableContainer`, **inline in the block loop**,
after the block state has been written and the block entity fetched, guarded on
`SharedConstants.DEBUG_STRUCTURE_EDIT_MODE` being false. So the container tag is
drawn **at placement, not at save**: `putLong` writes it into the info's own
`CompoundTag` and `loadWithComponents` reads that compound eight instructions
later. Nothing at save time draws anything;
`RandomizableContainer.trySaveLootTable` only decides whether to *write* the
value it was given, `if (getLootTableSeed() != 0L)`.

Nothing else in `placeInWorld` reads it, and the two candidates that look as
though they should are both answered:

* **The palette pick does not.** Offset 21 is
  `StructurePlaceSettings.getRandomPalette(this.palettes, offset)`, which uses
  the settings' own `random` field.
* **No processor does.** `StructurePlaceSettings.getRandom(pos)` returns that
  same field if set and otherwise `RandomSource.create(Mth.getSeed(pos))` — a
  fresh per-position stream. `SinglePoolElement.getSettings` never sets the
  field. And `CappedProcessor`, the one processor whose
  `evaluatesEntirePieceState` is true, seeds itself from
  `ServerLevel.getSeed()` (offset 73) before `Util.toShuffledList`.

`place_piece`'s own header already said this and said it correctly. What was
wrong was a comment 1,900 lines away in `tools/worldgen-assembly/src/generate.rs`,
which justified trial chambers not touching the mineshaft's stream with
"`place_piece` is template placement, which draws only inside its own
processors". The conclusion is right and the reason is not: what separates the
two streams is the per-structure reseed at offset 293, not any absence of draws.
Fixed in the same commit as this section.

### 44.3 The blast radius, as a count of call sites and structures rather than an impression

Five `place_piece` call sites, and the distribution is not four-of-a-kind:

| call site | structure | passes today | jigsaw |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `crates/rustmc-worldgen/src/structure/ruined_portal.rs::post_process` (`:673`) | `ruined_portal` (7 variants) | `Some` | no |
| `tools/worldgen-assembly/src/generate.rs` `place_nether_fossil_in_chunk` | `nether_fossil` | `None` | no |
| `tools/worldgen-assembly/src/generate.rs` `place_end_city_in_chunk` | `end_city` | `None` | no |
| `tools/worldgen-assembly/src/generate.rs` `place_village_in_chunk` | **`village_*` (5) and `trial_chambers`** | `None` | **yes** |
| `tools/worldgen-assembly/src/generate.rs` `place_shipwreck_in_chunk` | `shipwreck` | `None` | no |

**So "the jigsaw driver" is one call site, not four, and it reaches six
structures.** `place_village_in_chunk` is misnamed: trial chambers go through it
too, which is why the 89 are reachable by threading one function. The other three
`None` call sites are template pieces of non-jigsaw structures and each is its
own item.

And the other three do not need this stream for their chests at all, which is
worth knowing before anyone budgets them as the same work. `setBlockEntityLootTable`
has exactly **four** callers in the jar —
`ShipwreckPieces$ShipwreckPiece`, `EndCityPieces$EndCityPiece`,
`BonusChestFeature` and `MonsterRoomFeature` — and the first two take it in
`handleDataMarker`, from a `minecraft:structure_block` marker, not from the
template's own nbt. `place_piece` does not run `handleDataMarker` at all. So
shipwreck's and end city's containers are a **data-marker** item and only the
jigsaw call site is a **stream** item.

The seed each structure enters on, derived by `structure_step_indices()` in the
order `ResourceManagerRegistryLoadTask` imposes and corroborated for `mineshaft`
by a figure measured out of vanilla's own corridors:

```
trial_chambers   underground_structures (step 3)  index 4
village_desert   surface_structures     (step 4)  index 21
village_plains   surface_structures     (step 4)  index 22
village_savanna  surface_structures     (step 4)  index 23
village_snowy    surface_structures     (step 4)  index 24
village_taiga    surface_structures     (step 4)  index 25
```

### 44.4 Why the residual was predicted to move, and the three reasons it should not

A stream position is only observable through a consumer, so the question is what
consumes this stream **after** the containers. In the jigsaw chain, three
candidates and all three are answered:

* **`Structure.afterPlace`** gets the same random at `placeInChunk` offset 147.
  It is overridden by exactly two classes in the jar,
  `WoodlandMansionStructure` and `DesertPyramidStructure`. **Neither is a
  jigsaw structure**, so for a village or a chamber it is the base no-op.
* **`handleDataMarker`** gets it in `SinglePoolElement.place` at offset 105.
  `StructurePoolElement.handleDataMarker` is `return` — one instruction — and
  `SinglePoolElement` does not override it. The ten classes that do are all
  `TemplateStructurePiece` subclasses, none of them a pool element.
* **`FeaturePoolElement.place`** does consume it, and heavily: its whole body is
  `this.feature.value().place(level, generator, random, pos)`. **This is the
  only path by which the piece stream can move a block state in a jigsaw
  structure.** Of vanilla's 188 `data/minecraft/worldgen/template_pool/*.json`,
  **14 hold a `minecraft:feature` element**: `ancient_city/sculk` and 13 village
  `decor`/`trees` pools. `trial_chambers` holds none. And in this port a feature
  pool element is refused out loud and places nothing, so it consumes nothing
  either.

Which gives the prediction this section exists to test, and it is the opposite
of §43's: **threading the write stream should move zero block states**, because
in this port no consumer of it writes a block. §43's worked counter-example,
`RuinedPortalPiece.postProcess` — where `spreadNetherrack`'s
`nextInt(max(1, 8 - avgSpan/2))` is the very next draw and sets the apron radius
— is a **non-jigsaw** structure, and it is the one call site that already
threads.

`RUSTMC_JIGSAW_STREAM` is unset by default, so nothing above changed any output
yet. `ci/jigsaw-stream-cost.sh` measures the three arms off one binary; its
`off`-against-`stream` figure is ours against ours, so it needs no floor and a
zero in it is a zero. What that came back as is the next subsection.

### 44.5 Measured: zero block states move, and the draws were demonstrably spent

`ci/jigsaw-stream-cost.sh v2`, branch `worldgen-jigsaw-random` at `f1b894bb`
off `worldgen-block-entities` `70d3f1609` (was `79a5db12`, the same patch-id before a rebase rewrote it), Threadripper, 2026-08-17. Seed 12345,
centre chunk (64,64), r=16, 1,089 chunks, bbox `48 48 80 80`, `RUSTMC_WORKERS=1`,
`RUSTMC_DECORATE_ORDER=zx` read off the references' own `HARVEST-ORDER` stamps
(ORACLE rule 29). **One binary, three arms.**

**THE PRIMARY FIGURE, ours against ours:**

```
off vs stream    (the draws alone)                    mismatches 0 of 107,053,056
off vs faithful  (the draws plus both start orders)   mismatches 0 of 107,053,056
stream vs faithful (the orders alone)                 mismatches 0 of 107,053,056
```

**It is deliberately stated as a count and carries no percentage.** Both sides
of each pair are this generator one environment variable apart, so this is an
identity between two of our own outputs, not a parity level, and printing it as
a nine-nines exactness would put a number in this document that a reader could
mistake for a score against vanilla. There is nothing to compare it to and
nothing to clear: no reference is opened and no floor applies.

**And a zero here needed a positive control, because a pass with no draws in it
produces the same zero as a pass with no effect.** The trace the script requires:

```
off     arm:   0 trace lines,   0 draws
stream  arm:  52 trace lines, 121 draws, every group  structure=trial_chambers step=3 index=4
```

So the stream was threaded, 121 `nextLong`s were spent on it that the `off` arm
never spent, and the world did not move by one block. The three region trees are
byte-identical on disk, and here that is the **result** rather than a broken
instrument — what rules out an instrument that never ran is the 121 against the 0,
plus three separately-timed dump sections in the log.

Scored vanilla first against the stamped ensemble, all three arms are identical
to the block:

```
TWIN FLOOR (N=2, harvest rows, vanilla c vs vanilla d)   17,779 masked positions
admissible charge, arm off / stream / faithful            3,602 / 3,602 / 3,602
```

**That 3,602 is a DELTA BASELINE and not a level, and the arms agreeing is the
whole of what it says here.** N=2 on this window is the regime where the innocent
floor alone spans thousands of blocks; the script's header says so and this
section is not quoting it as an exactness. It is not comparable to the 7,940 at
N=5 in §17 — different N, and main has moved since.

### 44.6 What this window CANNOT say, from its own trace

The trace is also a coverage report, and it refuses two readings this measurement
would otherwise seem to support. **A gate a window cannot exercise proves
nothing.**

* **No village start reaches this window.** All 52 groups are `trial_chambers`.
  Village pools are the only ones in vanilla holding a `minecraft:feature`
  element and that is the single path by which this stream can move a block
  state, so **the arm that could have cost something was never run.** The
  village path needs seed 20260722, whose two savanna villages carry the 22
  unported `feature_pool_element`s `caveats()` already names —
  `ci/jigsaw-stream-villages.sh`, ours against ours, opening no reference
  because that window carries no quotable exactness figure at all (§15, §16).
* **Every group has `starts=1`, so the `faithful` arm reordered nothing.** Its
  zero is therefore not evidence that walking the starts in
  `StructureManager.startsForStructure` order is safe — the reorder had no two
  starts to swap. `faithful` stays behind the knob on that ground alone.

### 44.7 The affordability verdict, and the number it rests on

**Affordable, for trial chambers, at a measured cost of 0 block states of
107,053,056 on the one overworld window this project has a quotable exactness
figure on.** The trade §43 feared — "89 more positions at the cost of every r=16
floor" — is not the trade on offer: the floor is not touched, because the draws
this adds are read by nothing that writes a block.

What the recommendation does **not** cover, and neither should anyone reading it:

* **The villages.** Two independent reasons to leave them behind the knob. The
  measurement never reached one (§44.6), and even when it does they cannot be
  made *correct* while `FeaturePoolElement` is unported. Of the 14 pools in
  §44.4, **13 are village `decor`/`trees` pools and the 14th is
  `ancient_city/sculk`** — which does not matter here only because no ancient
  city is placed at all, so the village pools are the whole of the live
  exposure. A refused feature element leaves every later container in that
  start reading a stream vanilla has moved further along. A village's
  `LootTableSeed` is therefore only reproducible up to its first feature
  element, and that is a `FeaturePoolElement` item, not a stream item.
* **The start orders.** Untested here, for want of a chunk with two starts of
  one structure.

**And the cheapest finding of this branch was not the measurement.** Auditing
the draw against the jar turned up `suspicious_sand` and `suspicious_gravel` in
`is_randomizable_container`, which are not `RandomizableContainer`s at all —
`BrushableBlockEntity extends BlockEntity`. Trial chambers hold suspicious
gravel, so those two spurious draws per block would have put every later
container in the chamber on the wrong `LootTableSeed` **while every block-state
instrument in this repo reported zero**, because a spurious `nextLong` writes no
block. It cost nothing while every jigsaw call site passed `random: None` and it
would have cost the whole of this section the moment one did. The falsifier is
`a_suspicious_block_is_not_a_randomizable_container_and_spends_no_draw`.

### 44.8 What the draws actually buy: 60, not 89 — and eight of them come out wrong on one key

`ci/jigsaw-stream-bediff.sh v1`, same window, same reference, vanilla first,
`RUSTMC_RECORD_BLOCK_ENTITIES=1` in both arms, `63eb8bc2`, Threadripper.

```
arm off      vanilla_be=173 ours_be=72   missing=104 spurious=3 id=0 payload=0 exact=69
arm stream   vanilla_be=173 ours_be=132  missing=44  spurious=3 id=0 payload=8 exact=121
```

Emitted 72 -> 132, of which recorded 41 -> 101, on 121 draws over 52
`trial_chambers` groups. **The union-based exactness the tool also prints is
again not quoted here, for §43.3's reason: 44 of the 176 are classes not ported
at all, so it is a progress bar.**

**§43's closing criterion was "missing down by 89 with `payload=0 id=0` still
holding", and BOTH halves of it are refuted.**

* **It is 60, not 89.** `missing` falls 104 -> 44 and `exact` rises 69 -> 121.
  The 89 over-attributed: what is still missing is **13 `vault` and 7
  `brushable_block`** and the remainder, and none of those was ever reachable
  by this stream — neither class is a `RandomizableContainer`, so
  `placeInWorld` spends no draw on either and no amount of threading produces
  one. The 89 counted every unreproduced position inside a trial chamber; the
  stream reaches only the containers among them.
* **`payload` goes 0 -> 8, and every one is `decorated_pot.sherds`.** This is a
  genuine regression in the condition §43 closed on, and it is the honest cost
  of making a class emittable: `DecoratedPotBlockEntity.saveAdditional` writes
  `store("sherds", PotDecorations.CODEC, decorations)` whenever `decorations`
  is not `PotDecorations.EMPTY`, and `decorations` is loaded straight from the
  template's own `sherds`. The record this branch makes carries `LootTable` and
  the seed and **not** `sherds`, so the payload is right on the loot keys and
  missing one key, which `bediff` correctly scores as a mismatch rather than an
  absence.

**Note which way that last one cuts.** The emitter's `incomplete` mechanism
exists to refuse a payload wrong on one key, and it did not fire here — because
`incomplete` asks whether the *placer* could supply what it drew, and the placer
could: it had a table and a seed. What it did not have is a key belonging to a
different part of the same tag. So `incomplete` is not a general guard against
partial payloads, only against seedless loot, and this is the first measurement
to show the difference. `EmitStats::incomplete` is 0 in both arms.

**The §42.5 identity holds under the threaded stream**: block states across the
two recording arms are `mismatches 0 of 107,053,056`, which is also the third
independent confirmation of §44.5 — the same zero, taken with recording on
rather than off, on different dumps.

### 44.9 The village arm, on the window that can exercise it: also zero, and the reason is not the same reason

`ci/jigsaw-stream-villages.sh v1 16`, seed **20260722**, centre chunk (64,64),
r=16, `1e7fe2b5`, Threadripper. **Ours against ours, no reference opened, so no
level is produced and none could be** — that window carries no quotable
exactness figure at all (§15, §16).

```
off vs stream       mismatches 0 of 107,053,056
off vs faithful     mismatches 0 of 107,053,056
stream vs faithful  mismatches 0 of 107,053,056
```

And this window does reach the village path, which §44.6 said the seed-12345 one
does not:

```
village_savanna    groups=89   draws=3     groups-with-2+-starts=0
trial_chambers     groups=126  draws=289   groups-with-2+-starts=0
feature pool elements refused: 4x minecraft:pile_hay, 2x minecraft:acacia
```

**So the affordability answer now covers the path that could have broken it.** A
village start with an unported `feature_pool_element` in it is present, six of
them are refused, three container draws are spent in savanna villages, and the
block states still do not move. That is the stronger form of §44.5: the draws are
unobservable in block states even where the desync sites are real.

**What is STILL unexercised, on both windows, and must not be read as covered:**
`groups-with-2+-starts=0` in every arm of every run. No chunk in either window
holds two starts of one structure, so the `faithful` arm's `LongOpenHashSet`
walk has never had two starts to reorder. **Its three zeros are consistent with
the order being safe and are not evidence of it**, and that is the whole reason
`RUSTMC_JIGSAW_STREAM=2` stays a separate setting from `=1` rather than being
folded into it.

**And the three savanna draws are still not CORRECT, only harmless.** §44.7's
second reservation stands unchanged: a refused feature element leaves every later
container in that start reading a stream vanilla has moved further along, so a
village's `LootTableSeed` is reproducible only up to its first feature element.
Six refusals in this window is six desync points. What §44.9 adds is that this
costs nothing in *block states* — it is a wrong payload risk, not a wrong world
risk, and it is why the recommendation is trial chambers only.

### 44.10 The `sherds` regression is gone, and the villages' desync is ONE draw of 292 — the two numbers §44.7's recommendation was missing

Two facts stood between §44.7 and a decision the owner could actually take,
and both are measured here rather than reasoned about.

**One: `payload` is back to 0.** §44.8 recorded `payload` going 0 -> 8 under
the stream and called it "a genuine regression in the condition §43 closed
on". `0f92e0e9` carried the template's `sherds` in answer to it and **nothing
re-ran the comparison**, so the branch's own documentation still stated a live
regression that had already been fixed. Re-run on the same window, same
reference, vanilla first, `ci/jigsaw-stream-bediff.sh v2-postsherds`,
`09e0b08d5` (was `24ad1732`, the same patch-id before a rebase rewrote it):

```
arm off      vanilla_be=173 ours_be=72   missing=104 spurious=3 id=0 payload=0 exact=69
arm stream   vanilla_be=173 ours_be=132  missing=44  spurious=3 id=0 payload=0 exact=129
```

Against §44.8's `payload=8 exact=121`: **`payload` 8 -> 0 and `exact`
121 -> 129**, the eight being exactly the eight decorated pots. `missing`
is unchanged at 44 and `id` unchanged at 0. The positive control held in the
direction it must — `off` traced 0 draws, `stream` traced 121 — and the block
states across the two recording arms are `mismatches 0 of 107,053,056`.

**So §43's closing criterion is now refuted on one half and satisfied on the
other**, where §44.8 refuted both: the stream buys 60 and not 89, and
`payload=0 id=0` does hold.

**Two: exactly one draw in the whole 20260722 window is desynced.**
§44.7 reserves the villages on a mechanism — a refused `feature_pool_element`
spends none of the placed feature's draws, so every *later* container in that
stream reads a position vanilla has moved past — and §44.9 counts the
refusals (six) and the draws (three) without ever relating them. The two
orderings have opposite consequences and the count was never taken. It is now,
by `StreamAudit`, which records the running draw count at the instant of the
first refusal in a stream:

```
  village_savanna    groups=89   draws=3    refusals=6  downstream_draws=1
  trial_chambers     groups=126  draws=289  refusals=0  downstream_draws=0
                                 total 292             of which downstream: 1
```

**Trial chambers hold no feature pool element, so their 289 draws cannot be
downstream of anything — the zero there is structural and not a sample.** Of
the villages' three draws, two are spent before the first refusal in their
stream and are as correct as a chamber's; **one is spent after it**, and that
one lands on a `LootTableSeed` vanilla does not produce.

**What this does to the recommendation.** §44.7 says "the recommendation is
trial chambers only", and the knob has no trial-chambers-only setting:
`RUSTMC_JIGSAW_STREAM=1` is global across every jigsaw structure
(`JigsawStream::from_env`, `tools/worldgen-assembly/src/generate.rs`). So the
action available to the owner is not the recommendation as worded, and the
gap between them is now a number rather than a caveat: flipping the default
buys 60 positions and 0 block states of 107,053,056 on both windows, and
costs **one wrong `LootTableSeed`** on the window that can exercise villages
at all. It is a wrong payload rather than a wrong world, and it is one.

**Still unexercised and still not covered**, unchanged from §44.9:
`groups-with-2+-starts=0` in every arm of every run, so `faithful`'s reorder
has never had two starts to swap and stays a separate setting on that ground.

## 45. The nine remaining block-entity classes are arms of one save, not nine ports — the four containers land together (2026-08-18)

§42.1 measured that one of the ten classes is a function of the finished block
state and called the other nine "one item rather than nine". §43 built the
channel they were waiting on and §44 threaded the stream that lets the
containers spend their draw. This is the first arm taken on the strength of
that claim, and the claim holds in the strongest available form: **one arm
serves four of the nine classes**, because in the 26.2 jar
`Chest`, `Barrel`, `Dispenser` and `HopperBlockEntity.saveAdditional` are the
same three instructions.

```
    super.saveAdditional(out)
    if (!trySaveLootTable(out)) ContainerHelper.saveAllItems(out, items)
```

The hopper adds `putInt("TransferCooldown", cooldownTime)` **after** that
if/else and outside it, so a loot-bearing hopper writes it too, and
`cooldownTime` is 0 on an instance nothing has ticked.

### 45.1 The half that had to come from the bytecode

Whether an all-empty container writes `Items:[]` or omits the key entirely is
the whole difference between this arm and a wrong payload, and the census
data could not settle it: all five bare barrels and all five hoppers on the
window carry an empty list, which is equally consistent with "written empty"
and with "these five happen to be empty".

`ContainerHelper.saveAllItems(out, items)` delegates to the three-argument
form with `iconst_1`, and that form ends:

```
    66: invokeinterface TypedOutputList.isEmpty:()Z
    72: ifeq 87
    75: iload_2                 // alwaysWrite
    76: ifne 87                 // set -> jump over the discard
    79: ldc "Items"
    82: invokeinterface ValueOutput.discard:(Ljava/lang/String;)V
```

So the key is discarded **only** when the list is empty *and* `alwaysWrite` is
false, and the two-argument form every one of these four classes calls passes
it true. `Items:[]` is written.

### 45.2 What it measured, and what it does NOT overturn

`ci/jigsaw-stream-bediff.sh besave-containers`, seed 12345, r=16, centre
(64,64), bbox 48 48 80 80, dump order `zx` matched to the reference's
`HARVEST-ORDER`, vanilla first, `62939b9b`:

```
  arm off      vanilla_be=173 ours_be=72   missing=104 spurious=3 id=0 payload=0 exact=69
  arm stream   vanilla_be=173 ours_be=142  missing=34  spurious=3 id=0 payload=0 exact=139
```

Against §44.10's `missing=44 exact=129` on the same arm: **`missing` 44 -> 34
and `exact` 129 -> 139, with `payload` and `id` both still 0.** The ten are
the five bare barrels and the five hoppers, and `barrel` and `hopper` leave
the missing-by-id table entirely. Block states across the two recording arms
are `mismatches 0 of 107,053,056`; the positive control held (`off` 0 draws,
`stream` 121).

**§42.1's null is not overturned.** It says: do not emit a constant payload
for a `hopper` or a bare `barrel` however tempting the 5/5 uniformity looks,
because both classes can carry loot and `{Items:[]}` for one that does is a
*wrong* payload. That is still true and this arm does not do it. What makes
the emission right here is that **a record exists** — the channel saw the
placement and the template's tag named no `LootTable` — so the payload is read
off the placement rather than guessed from a count of five. The null was drawn
on exactly that distinction and this is the case it was drawn to allow.

### 45.3 What is left, and it is two shapes rather than five classes

```
  13  minecraft:vault             template tag: `config`
   7  minecraft:trial_spawner     template tag: `normal_config`, `ominous_config`
   7  minecraft:brushable_block   a draw, and not the containers' draw
   4  minecraft:potent_sulfur     already derivable — these are the r=16 edge
   3  minecraft:chest             recorded, but their piece never ran
```

The first two are one shape: a key the template already carries and this port
throws away at parse time. The remaining `chest` and `potent_sulfur` counts
are not class gaps.

### 45.4 The template tag is not a draw, and conflating the two hid seven classes

The recording added in §43 lived **inside** the guard on the container draw:

```rust
if info.has_nbt && is_randomizable_container(block_of(&state)) {
    if let Some(random) = random.as_deref_mut() { /* draw, then record */ }
}
```

Two things are conflated there and they are not the same kind of thing at all.
The **draw** is `placeInWorld`'s and is parity-relevant: only a
`RandomizableContainer` carrying an `nbt` spends a `nextLong`, and only when a
stream was threaded to spend it from. The **record** is not an event — a
template's tag is data the piece already holds, and carrying it costs no draw,
changes no block and needs no stream.

Nesting the second inside the first had two consequences nobody had separated:

* **`vault`, `trial_spawner` and every other non-container class could never
  be recorded** — not for want of a port, but because they are not
  `RandomizableContainer`s and so never reached the branch that records.
* **No class at all was recorded with `RUSTMC_JIGSAW_STREAM` unset**, which is
  the default and the setting every published block-state figure was taken at.

The tag now rides whole (`TemplateBlock::payload`, `Info::payload`) rather than
as keys plucked one per class — the pattern §42.1 predicted would not
terminate, and which by §44.8 had already grown from `LootTable` to `sherds`
with `config`, `normal_config` and `ominous_config` still to come. Vanilla does
not pluck: `placeInWorld` hands the whole compound to `loadWithComponents` and
the block entity keeps what it understands. So **one** place decides what
survives, `block_entity::saved_template_keys`, and a class with no entry there
keeps nothing — the default is an absence rather than a guess.

### 45.5 The saved `config` is the template's minus every field the codec omits

Carrying the tag through unfiltered is the failure that rule exists to stop,
and the vault is where it showed: copying the template's `config` verbatim
scored **nine of thirteen** as a payload mismatch on that one key.

The template is not the saved tag. The block entity loads it and
`saveAdditional` re-encodes, and `VaultConfig`'s codec omits a field whose
value equals the default it was built with. Read instruction by instruction,
it is **per field rather than per class**:

```text
  loot_table                      lenientOptionalFieldOf(name, DEFAULT.lootTable)
  activation_range                lenientOptionalFieldOf(name, DEFAULT.activationRange)
  deactivation_range              lenientOptionalFieldOf(name, DEFAULT.deactivationRange)
  key_item                        fieldOf   — no optional wrapper at all
  override_loot_table_to_display  lenientOptionalFieldOf(name)   (the Optional form)
```

So `key_item` is always written and the three defaulted fields are not, which
**is the 9/4 split exactly**: nine plain vaults save `{key_item}` and four
ominous ones save `{key_item, loot_table}` because their table is not the
default. Confirmed off both sides at `(1051,-39,1260)` rather than argued —
ours carried
`{key_item:{count:1,id:trial_key}, loot_table:chests/trial_chambers/reward}`
and vanilla saves `{key_item:{count:1,id:trial_key}}`. The defaults are
`VaultConfig`'s private no-arg constructor:
`BuiltInLootTables.TRIAL_CHAMBERS_REWARD`, `4.0d`, `4.5d`.

**A count could not have found this and a correlation would have got it
backwards.** `config:compound=13` on all thirteen with `key_item`/`loot_table`
inside it on four (§42.1) reads equally well as "nine vaults have an empty
config" — which is the answer this branch first implemented, and it was wrong
in the other direction.

### 45.6 What the arms measure, and the two shapes still open

`ci/jigsaw-stream-bediff.sh besave-confirm`, seed 12345, r=16, centre (64,64),
bbox 48 48 80 80, dump order `zx` matched to the reference's `HARVEST-ORDER`,
vanilla first, recording on in both arms.

```text
                       vanilla_be  ours_be  missing  spurious  id  payload  exact
  §43   (channel)         173         71      105       3       0     0       68
  §44.10 (stream)         173        132       44       3       0     0      129
  45.2  (containers)      173        142       34       3       0     0      139
  45.6  arm off           173        102       74       3       0     0       99
  45.6  arm stream        173        162       14       3       0     0      159
```

**`payload=0` and `id=0` hold at every step, and that is the condition rather
than the headline.** `exact` 129 -> 159 on the stream arm is thirty positions:
five bare barrels, five hoppers, thirteen vaults and seven trial spawners.
`missing` 44 -> 14.

**The `off` arm is the one worth reading twice.** It moves 105 -> 74 without a
single draw being spent anywhere, because §45.4 separated the record from the
draw: `vault` and `trial_spawner` carry no loot and never needed a stream, and
until this branch they could not be recorded at all. **Those positions are
therefore independent of the `RUSTMC_JIGSAW_STREAM` decision §44.7 leaves to
the owner** — they land at the default setting.

The block states are unchanged by all of it:
`mismatches 0 of 107,053,056` across the two recording arms, and
`ci/be-channel-identical.sh` GREEN at (61,50) r=4 — recording on and off
byte-identical.

**What is left is two shapes, not five classes:**

* **`brushable_block` (7) — not a class gap at all.** They belong to
  `minecraft:ocean_ruin_cold`, and ocean ruins do not go through
  `structure::place::place_piece`; `EmitStats` reports `orphaned 0 incomplete
  0`, so no record is made to refuse. `saved_template_keys` carries the arm
  they will need — `BrushableBlockEntity.saveAdditional` is
  `if (!trySaveLootTable(out) && !item.isEmpty()) store("item", ...)`, and it
  is the one class allowed to take `LootTableSeed` from its tag, because it is
  not a `RandomizableContainer` and no draw is spent on it (§44.7) — but that
  arm is **unexercised on this window and must not be read as measured.**
* **`chest` (2 missing, and a third deliberately given up) and `decorated_pot`
  / `dispenser` in the `off` arm — a draw, not a class.** These are §43.5's
  item and §44's answer: they are emittable exactly when the piece stream is
  threaded, which is the owner's call.
* **`potent_sulfur` 4 missing / 3 spurious** is the r=16 edge and predates all
  of this; it is not a block-entity gap.

**Killed as a null — do not re-run:** do not look for a ninth and tenth
`complete` arm. Nine of the ten classes are served by four arms — the
containers' shared save, the spawner's constants, the vault's config
normalisation and the brushable's tag-carried seed — and the tenth
(`potent_sulfur`) is derived. What is missing from the window is now a
placement path and a draw, neither of which is an arm.

---

## 55. What worldgen parity may honestly be stated as, today — the inventory, and why the aggregate is still refused (2026-08-12)

The gameplay axis has denominators and is getting a composite figure. This
section is the worldgen answer to the same question, and it is **a reading of
`ci/figures.toml` rather than a measurement campaign** — with one exception,
§55.4, which cost no generation at all.

Three things are established here. **One:** worldgen has a quotable exactness
figure again, `RESUME-STATE`'s standing headline to the contrary, and it is
99.998805%. **Two:** exactly two current, order-controlled, unmodified-baseline
exactness levels exist across the whole axis, they are on different seeds, and
they do not compose — **no aggregate worldgen parity percentage is defensible
today.** **Three:** the ledger's stated reason for the nether's share of that —
*there is no N at which it would carry a level claim* — is drawn from one of two
floor estimators that face opposite ways in N, and does not survive being taken
on the other. The refusal survives; its mechanism does not, and the corrected
mechanism turns a closed question into a half-hour open one.

### 33.1 The premise this section was written on is refuted by the tree

The item was briefed as: *the project's one quotable worldgen exactness figure
fell below its own floor, leaving no aggregate parity percentage for the whole
axis — and that is still true.* The first half is history and the second half
is false. That headline was written on tip `8c7e024c`, where seed 12345 read
**1,329 against an innocent floor of 141..2,241 at N=15** — 0.59x, below its
own floor, §23.5. Commit `c8178dc7` — §24, the reference-costing ladder — then
did the thing §24.3 costed at about an hour of box time, widened the ensemble
to a pool of 40, and the same window re-anchored at **1,279 against a floor of
0..362 at N=39**. That is 3.53x, and it is registered as
`ow-r16-12345-level-n39-iceberg`.

**So the axis has a headline: 1,279 of 107,053,056 — 99.998805%.** Seed 12345,
overworld, r=16, centre (64,64), 1,089 chunks, full height, nothing masked,
vanilla first, dump order matched to the references' own `HARVEST-ORDER`,
against 39 unmodified-vanilla references, floor 0..362, p_hide 7.4%.

This is the sixth consecutive item whose brief was refuted by its first read of
the tree, and it is worth naming the mechanism: `RESUME-STATE` records a
*state*, the ledger records *figures*, and the ledger moved four commits after
the state was written. Read the ledger.

### 33.2 The inventory — what is quotable NOW

47 figures on the worldgen axis: 34 `worldgen`, 13 `worldgen-floor`. Twelve
carry `quotable = true` **and** a percentage, which is what a downstream
consumer enumerating the ledger mechanically would pick up. **Only two of those
twelve are current exactness levels of this port against unmodified vanilla.**

| figure | % | residual | floor | N | verdict now, and the single thing that decides it |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `ow-r16-12345-level-n39-iceberg` | 99.998805% | 1,279 | 362 | 39 | **QUOTABLE** — 3.53x, matched order, unmasked, full height |
| `end-r16-n6-exact` | 100.000000% | 0 | 252 | 6 | **QUOTABLE** — residual is zero, so no floor can swamp it |
| `ow-r16-12345-level-n15-iceberg-before` | 99.997088% | 3,117 | 2,241 | 15 | superseded — an earlier rung of the same window |
| `ow-r16-12345-level-n15-lakeice` | 99.997092% | 3,113 | 2,241 | 15 | superseded — an earlier rung of the same window |
| `ow-r16-12345-level-n15-lakeice-before` | 99.996133% | 4,140 | 2,241 | 15 | superseded — an earlier rung of the same window |
| `ow-r16-12345-admissible-iceberg` | 99.992583% | 7,940 | 4,835 | 5 | superseded — an earlier rung of the same window |
| `ow-r16-12345-admissible-topmaterial` | 99.992804% | 7,704 | 4,835 | 5 | **stale** — its own provenance says `RESTACKED AND NOT RE-MEASURED` |
| `readme-restates-ow-r16-admissible` | 99.972345% | 29,605 | 29,560 | 2 | restates a figure the ledger disqualifies for **uncontrolled chunk order** |
| `ow-r16-floor-n2` | 99.972388% | 29,560 | — | 2 | **is a floor, not our exactness** — vanilla against itself |
| `nether-r16-floor-n2` | 99.109624% | 317,725 | — | 2 | **is a floor, not our exactness** — vanilla against itself |
| `nether-r7-ssv-core` | 99.8853% | 8,459 | 48 | 2 | `harvest = unrecorded`, r=7, y 0..127, twin+order masked |
| `nether-r7-basalt-core` | 99.9158% | 6,127 | 95,240 | 2 | `harvest = unrecorded`, and quotable only as an order-intersected core |

Four hazards in that table are worth stating as rules, because each is a way a
careful reader still gets a wrong number:

**A floor is not an exactness figure, and two of them are shaped exactly like
one.** `ow-r16-floor-n2` and `nether-r16-floor-n2` carry `percent` fields —
99.972388% and 99.109624% — because a floor expressed over its denominator has
a percentage form. They measure **vanilla against itself**. Quoting either as
parity would publish vanilla's own irreproducibility as this server's fidelity,
and the nether one would do it at 99.1%, which is low enough to look like a
candid admission rather than a category error.

**`quotable = true` on a restatement means the restatement is faithful, not
that the number may be quoted** — and the worked example this section was
drafted around has been closed since, which is worth recording rather than
deleting. `readme-restates-ow-r16-admissible` restates 99.972345%, whose source
entry `ow-r16-admissible-n2` is `quotable = false`, disqualifier *uncontrolled
chunk order*. For three days the restatement carried `quotable = true` anyway.
The README prose was correct throughout — it calls the number **not quotable**
in the same sentence — so nothing published was ever wrong; what was wrong is
that the flag and the prose disagreed, in the half a consumer enumerating this
ledger would believe. The window-match rule could not catch it because that
entry's `compare_keys` **omitted `dump_order`**, the one key on which the source
had been disqualified: the discriminating key was missing from the comparison
that exists to discriminate.

**Both halves of that are fixed on `main` and this section is the after
picture, not the before one.** Rule 9 (`RESTATED-QUOTABILITY`) refuses a
restatement that out-ranks its source, and the entry is now `quotable = false`;
rule 10 (`RESTATED-WINDOW`) requires a republishing entry's `compare_keys` to
cover **every** key its source's window records, and that entry now compares all
eleven. Rule 10 then caught this section's own restatement,
`parity-restates-ow-r16-12345-n39`, which had been written comparing ten of
twelve keys with `order` and `p_hide` omitted — the identical shape, one day
later, by an author who had just written the paragraph above. It was corrected
by **completing the comparison, not by widening the rule**: all twelve keys were
read first and all twelve agree, so the restatement is the same measurement and
none of its numbers moved. Had `order` differed it would have been a different
measurement wearing the same number, and the entry would have been withdrawn
instead.

**`RESTACKED ... NOT RE-MEASURED` is free text and nothing enforces it.** It
appears seven times in `ci/figures.toml`. `ci/check-figures.py` never reads
`provenance` at all, so a figure can say in its own record that it was rebased
past 708 lines of worldgen it never saw and still pass green as `quotable =
true`. There is no staleness notion in the checker.

**An unrecorded harvest order is not a controlled one.** The two quotable
nether r=7 cores were taken 2026-08-02, before chunk order was known to be a
condition of a comparison (§15, ORACLE rule 29), and their windows say
`harvest = unrecorded`. ORACLE rule 30: recoverable is not known.

### 33.3 The floor question — print the N, because the N is most of the answer

The brief for this item required that any aggregate print the N behind each
input, on the grounds that worldgen floors have moved dramatically with N. They
have moved more than that phrasing suggests, and in **both directions**:

| window | floor at N | | | estimator |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| overworld, seed 12345, r=16 | 4,835 @ N=5 | 2,241 @ N=15 | **362 @ N=39** | innocent leave-one-out |
| nether, seed 20260722, r=16 | 317,725 @ N=2 | 766,490 @ N=6 | **1,172,835 @ N=8** | union |
| the End, seed 20260722, r=16 | 0 @ N=2 | **252 @ N=6** | — | pairwise union |

The End's row is the one already written up (§5): a published floor of exactly
zero across two references became 252 across six, and the conclusion drawn from
the zero — *there is nowhere for a defect to hide* — did not survive it.

The overworld row **falls 13x** and the nether row **rises 3.7x** over
comparable moves. That looks like a fact about the two dimensions. It is not.

### 33.4 There are two floor families in this tree and they face opposite ways

**Read §4.4 before this subsection: the measurement is already there, and that
is the point.** Nothing below is a new reading of the nether. What is new is
noticing that §4.4 published **two** columns with opposite behaviour and that
only the pessimistic one reached the ledger and the roadmap.

* The **union** family — `ci/floor-ensemble.sh`, `ci/floor-ladder.py` —
  accumulates `union over j>=2 of pos(ref1, refj)`. Its own header says it: *a
  floor is the size of a union and can only grow with N.* **Every r=16 nether
  floor that reached `ci/figures.toml` came from it**: 317,725 at N=2, 668,532
  and 766,490 at N=6, 1,172,835 at N=8.
* The **innocent leave-one-out** family — `ci/window-resolving-power.py`,
  `ci/floor-census.py`, `ci/floor-anchor.py` — charges a held-out vanilla run
  against the spellings the others are known to produce,
  `|{ p : val_X(p) not in V_S(p) }|`. Adding a reference can only **add**
  spellings to `V_S(p)`, so it is monotonically non-increasing in N. **Every
  published seed-12345 overworld floor came from it**, and so does §4.4's
  second table, which nobody carried forward.

So `nether-r16-n6-matched`'s verdict — *there is no N at which it would carry a
level claim: the floor grows faster than the residual falls* — is a true
statement **about a union**. It is not a statement about the nether, and the
766,490-against-362 gap between the two dimensions' headline floors is three
orders of magnitude of **estimator**, not of world. §4.4's own admissible table
descends exactly as the overworld's does:

```text
    N    charge      innocent floor  min      med      max     margin
    2   197,250                 114,497  184,323  269,217      0.73x
    3   110,947                  70,822  123,926  197,334      0.56x
    4    96,855                  49,102   94,226  157,381      0.62x
    5    85,181                  37,951   80,241  111,015      0.77x
```

**That table was already in this file and the ledger records the other one.**

What this branch added is a reproduction and one refinement, both free.
`ci/nether-anchor-floor.sh` re-reads the `n(n-1)` pair matrix that already
exists — 72 `.pos` files left by the nether ladder — through
`ci/floor-anchor.py`, which needs `2n-3` of them, so **no reference was
generated and no diff was run.** Six references a..f, all harvested `rows`,
leave-one-out at N=5, anchors a/b, verified candidate-by-candidate against the
full 30-diff matrix rather than on summary statistics. It reproduces §4.4's
N=5 row **to the block** — `37,951 .. 111,015` median `80,241` — from a
different script and a different construction, which is the strongest thing
that can be said for a number nobody has re-derived since it was taken.

The refinement is our own charge. §4.4 prints **85,181** at N=5; scoring the
same dump against every one of the six 5-subsets gives

```text
    our charge   85,181 ..  92,226   median 89,104     (order-matched dump zx)
    unmatched   125,495 .. 134,523   median 130,724    (dump ours)
```

so 85,181 is the **best** 5-subset, not a typical one. A floor is quoted as its
worst innocent reading; a charge quoted as its best, beside it, flatters by
construction. **Use 89,104 against 111,015 — 0.80x — and not 85,181 against
111,015, which reads 0.77x for no reason but which subset was drawn.**

**The r=16 nether still carries no level claim**, and an exactness quoted
against a floor it sits under is corroboration, not a measurement. But 0.80x is
not the 9.7x-below-floor the ledger records, and the difference is the whole
item: **0.80x is where seed 12345 sat at N=15** — 1,329 against 2,241, 0.59x —
before §24.3's ladder took it to 3.53x at N=39. Whether the nether can make the
same trip is **an open question with four measured rungs and no stable answer**,
and §55.6 is that question rather than an answer to it. What can be said is
narrower and worth saying on its own: over 35,684,352 blocks the admissible
instrument cannot separate this server from an unmodified vanilla run of the
same seed. That is the README's construction for the 20260722 overworld, and it
is deliberately not a percentage.

Note the second row above. Scoring the unmatched dump charges 130,724 against
the matched dump's 89,104, so ORACLE rule 29 fires on this window as it did on
the overworld: **32% of the unmatched nether charge is chunk order.** Score the
matched dump.

### 33.5 The aggregate, refused — twice, and the second refusal is the useful one

Composing the best available figure per dimension, exactly as the ledger stands:

| dimension | seed | residual | denominator | floor | N | estimator |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| overworld | 12345 | 1,279 | 107,053,056 | 362 | 39 | leave-one-out |
| the End | 20260722 | 0 | 71,368,452 | 252 | 6 | union |
| nether | 20260722 | 79,334 | 34,917,862 | 766,490 | 6 | union |
| **aggregate** | mixed | **80,613** | **213,339,370** | **767,104** | mixed | **mixed** |

That aggregate is 99.962214% and **its floor exceeds its residual by 9.52x**,
so ORACLE rule 6 fires on the composite exactly as it fires on a component.
99.92% of the summed floor is contributed by the nether, which is 16.4% of the
summed window. **Refused.**

Now the same composition with the nether taken from §4.4's **admissible**
column instead of its union one, and every input held at a common N=5 — the
strongest honest version available:

| dimension | seed | residual | denominator | floor MAX | margin |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| overworld | 12345 | 7,940 | 107,053,056 | 4,835 | 1.64x |
| the End | 20260722 | 0 | 71,368,704 | 252 | exact |
| nether | 20260722 | 89,104 | 35,684,352 | 111,015 | 0.80x |
| **aggregate** | mixed | **97,044** | **214,106,112** | **116,102** | **0.84x** |

**Still refused — 99.954675%, against a summed floor it sits 1.20x under.** But
this is the refusal worth recording, because it is a near miss with one named
cause rather than a rout with a structural one.

Three further reasons no aggregate is defensible today, each sufficient on its
own and none fixed by more references:

1. **The seeds differ.** The overworld figure is seed 12345; the nether and End
   are 20260722. A worldgen parity percentage composed across seeds is not a
   measurement of a generator, it is an average of two experiments. §17: chunk
   order is 83% of the 20260722 residual and 6.7% of the 12345 one under the
   identical instrument and geometry — the seeds are not interchangeable.
2. **The instruments differ.** The overworld figure masks nothing over
   107,053,056 positions. The End's denominator is 71,368,452 against the
   window's 71,368,704 — it is **ensemble-masked**, and the 252 positions it
   excludes are exactly its floor. Summing a masked denominator with an
   unmasked one produces a denominator that is neither.
3. **The floors are not addable.** Sum-of-floors is only a floor for the union
   of the windows if every input was taken by one estimator at one N. Above,
   they are not — and that is precisely the error §55.4 exists to name. The
   aggregate rows are given a summed floor above as the **most generous
   arithmetic available**, and it refuses anyway; a correctly measured
   composite floor could only be worse.

**What may be said today, and it should be said this way:**

> This server generates the End byte-identically to unmodified vanilla over
> 71,368,704 blocks, and matches vanilla in the overworld at 99.998805% —
> 1,279 blocks of 107,053,056 at seed 12345, against a vanilla-against-itself
> floor of 362 at 39 references. **There is no aggregate worldgen parity
> percentage**, because the nether's r=16 window cannot yet separate this
> server from a vanilla run of the same seed, and a figure that averaged the
> three dimensions would hide that.

### 33.6 The one measurement that would change the answer

**Three to six more `rows` references for the r=16 nether, to settle a ladder
that four rungs cannot.** Everything else in this section is structural — seeds,
masks, addability, estimator families — and each is a reason to state the
dimensions separately rather than a reason to measure anything.

§4.4 gives the nether four rungs under the admissible instrument, and the
margin does not behave:

```text
    N        charge   floor MAX   margin
    2       197,250     269,217    0.73x
    3       110,947     197,334    0.56x
    4        96,855     157,381    0.62x
    5        85,181     111,015    0.77x   (0.80x on the median 5-subset)
```

It dips and recovers rather than trending, and a power fit is correspondingly
worthless. Fit all four rungs and `floor_max ~ N^-0.930` against
`charge ~ N^-0.904`: the ratio creeps as `N^+0.026` and the window would need
**N ~= 2.9e7** references to convict. Drop the N=2 rung and the same fit reads
`N^-1.110` against `N^-0.515`, the ratio climbs as `N^+0.595`, and the answer is
**N ~= 8**. One rung, seven orders of magnitude.

**So the honest statement is that the nether ladder cannot be extrapolated from
what exists, and neither of the two available conclusions is supported.** The
ledger's *there is no N at which it would* is drawn from the union estimator and
does not transfer. The pessimistic fit above is one rung away from the
optimistic one. §24 exists because somebody nearly extrapolated the overworld
from two dots; this is the same shape with four.

**What it costs to settle it is small, and that is why this is the item.** §4.5
prices a nether r=16 reference at ~70 s, and the box holds eight — six `rows`
and two `rows_rev`. The two `rows_rev` ones cannot be pooled in: §4.4 prices the
harvest-order term at **1.85x at N=2** on this window and
`ow-r16-20260722-floor-mixed-order` is the registered case of a mixed pool
making a floor strictly worse. So the spend is **six more `rows` references,
about seven minutes of boots**, plus the anchor diffs — `2n-3` since §24.7, so
about twenty of them rather than a quadratic matrix. Call it half an hour of box
time for rungs at N=6..11, which is enough to say whether the margin is trending
at all.

**Both outcomes are worth the half hour, and they are opposite.** If the margin
climbs, the nether is on the seed-12345 trajectory and the axis knows what
buying the dimension costs. If it stays flat near 0.67x, then our charge and the
floor are descending together, the r=16 nether is **permanently** unresolvable
by references, and the axis should stop spending on it and say so — the correct
statement about the nether would then be the indistinguishability claim in §55.4
and never a percentage. Either way the aggregate stays refused; what changes is
whether the refusal is provisional or final.

**What would NOT change the answer, so that nobody spends on it:** more
references for the seed-12345 overworld (already at 3.53x and the only quotable
level), anything at all in the End (residual is 0), and re-taking the 20260722
overworld (§19.7 disqualifies it from the opposite direction). And **not** a
sub-window chosen to avoid the floor — `ow-r16-12345-floor-subwindow` and
`ow-r16-20260722-floor-subwindow` are both registered as unquotable for
selecting on the same runs that validate them, and the y-band version of the
same move is the null in §24.4.

## 51. Every reference §6 names was destroyed, and the End's floor is 127 rather than 252 (2026-08-18)

§6 closes the End against six named references:

```text
  van-end-20260722-r16-a    125
  van-end-20260722-r16-b    125
  det-ens-end-c             171
  det-ens-end-d               0        <-- byte-identical
  det-ens-end-e              46
  det-ens-end-f             206
```

**None of those six directories exists.** Not in `~/rustmc/oracle-work`, not in
any of the ~150 lane checkouts on the box, not under any other name: a search
for `det-ens*` and `van-end-*-r16*` across every `oracle-work` on the machine
returns nothing. The same is true of the six-reference stable set behind
`end-r16-n6-exact`, the figure `ci/figures.toml` calls *"the strongest figure
the project has and the only one that needs no qualifier"*. They went in the
2026-08-07 disk failure with everything else §5 of `PARITY.md` rested on, and
unlike the overworld's and the nether's they were never re-harvested, because
a dimension reading 100.000000% never looked like it needed anything.

So until today **every published End number was unreproducible**, and the
strongest claim in the project was the one with nothing behind it. That is not
a statement that the numbers were wrong. It is a statement that nobody could
have found out either way.

The two survivors are `van-end-20260722-c8362` and its `-b` twin, harvested
2026-07-30. They cover chunks 74..92 x 53..71 — r=9 at centre (83,62), 361
chunks — which is the wider generation the 225-chunk `end-city-window-isolated-model`
window was carved out of, so that figure's window at least still exists. But
both predate `HARVEST-ORDER`, so neither states the order it was generated in,
and §15 has been clear since 2026-08-10 that an unstamped reference cannot
carry a figure. `ci/end-biome-window.sh` refuses them by the same rule it
refuses anything else: read the order, never assert it.

**Fourteen replacements, all order-stamped**, seed 20260722, `GEN_ORDER=rows`
(`dump_order=zx`), default pool:

```text
  gen-wide-20260722-r16-end-{a..f}       r=16 c(64,64)  1,089 chunks
  gen-wide-20260722-r16-end-rr-{a,b}     r=16 c(64,64)  GEN_ORDER=rows_rev
  van-end-20260722-c0_0-open-{a,b,c}     r=7  c(0,0)      225 chunks
  van-end-20260722-c8362-open-{a,b,c}    r=7  c(83,62)    225 chunks
```

`jvm_opts=` on every one, so these are **default-pool references and the floors
below are UNCLAMPED floors**, carried with their N and never subtracted. §46
settles why that is the only honest form: `-Dmax.bg.threads=1` sets a
`ForkJoinPool`'s parallelism rather than a thread cap and does not clamp a
harvest at all.

**What reproduces, and what does not.**

Our dump against the fresh N=6 ensemble, vanilla first, `--ybox 0 255`, over the
same 71,368,704 blocks:

```text
   N=2  residual=0   floor=0
   N=3  residual=0   floor=127
   N=4  residual=0   floor=127
   N=5  residual=0   floor=127
   N=6  residual=0   floor=127
```

The **residual reproduces exactly**: 0 at every N, byte-identical to
`gen-wide-20260722-r16-end-a` unmasked, and the twin mask drops 0 positions
because there is nothing for it to drop. §6's central claim survives its own
references being destroyed, which is the best outcome that was available.

The **floor does not**. 252 was published; a same-order ensemble of six gives
**127**, and the pairwise numbers against the primary are 0, 127, 46, 46, 0
rather than 0, 46, 81, 125, 171, 206. The old set's spread is the signature of
an ensemble whose members were harvested in **different orders** — `harvest =
"unrecorded"` on all four End entries in `ci/figures.toml` says nobody knew
which — and §49 measures what that costs on this dimension: 944 blocks. 252 is
therefore best read as a mixed-order floor and 127 as a matched-order one, and
they are not two measurements of one quantity. Neither is subtracted from
anything.

The two r=7 windows have a floor of **0** at N=3, which is a real zero and not
the lucky one §5 corrected: three runs, not two, and the number of positions
where any of them disagrees is none.

## 49. An End floor is one biome's chorus plants: 25.8% of the window carries 100% of it (2026-08-18)

§47 found that 15.6% of the r=16 nether window is `basalt_deltas` and carries
98.9% of vanilla's own floor. The End has the same shape with a different
culprit, and it is sharper.

**Vanilla against vanilla, r=16, centre (64,64), seed 20260722, `--ybox 0 255`,
`--by-biome`** — the two same-order references that disagree, `a` against `c`,
127 positions:

```text
    biome                     blocks   mismatch   exactness     win%     res%
    end_highlands           18415616        123  99.999332%  25.80%  96.85%
    end_midlands            25821184          4  99.999985%  36.18%   3.15%
    small_end_islands       19464192          0 100.000000%  27.27%   0.00%
    end_barrens              7667712          0 100.000000%  10.74%   0.00%
```

Every one of the 127 is `chorus_plant` or `chorus_flower` against `air`, in
both directions, in five chunks, in y58..80. There is nothing else in an End
floor at all — no terrain, no carver, no ore, because the End has none of those
that vary.

**Why it is those two biomes and no others**, from vanilla's own feature lists
rather than from the residual:

```text
  the_end            end_spike, end_platform
  end_highlands      end_gateway_return, chorus_plant
  end_midlands       (none)
  small_end_islands  end_island_decorated
  end_barrens        (none)
```

`chorus_plant` is placed in `end_highlands` alone, with a
`count uniform 0..4` and a `MOTION_BLOCKING` heightmap. A chorus plant grows
upward from the surface across chunk boundaries, so the block it reads to
decide where to stop belongs to a neighbour that may not have decorated yet —
which is precisely the coupling `basalt_deltas` has in the nether. The four
positions charged to `end_midlands` are plants rooted in `end_highlands` whose
tops cross into it, which is why that biome shows a floor while placing no
feature of its own.

**The order knob, and it is the cleanest control this project has.** Same
window, same seed, vanilla against vanilla, `rows` against `rows_rev`:

```text
  rows-a      vs rows_rev-a     944   100.0% end_highlands
  rows_rev-a  vs rows_rev-b     317   100.0% end_highlands
  rows-a      vs rows-b           0
```

And now ours, which is the point:

```text
  ours (RUSTMC_DECORATE_ORDER=zx)  vs rows-a         0
  ours (RUSTMC_DECORATE_ORDER=zx)  vs rows_rev-a   944
```

**944 against 944, to the block.** Our dump differs from a reverse-order vanilla
by exactly what a forward-order vanilla differs from it by, because our dump
*is* a forward-order vanilla, bit for bit. So the End's order sensitivity is
0 against 944 — the sister nether window's is 335 against 7,638 — and a figure
taken against a reference whose order was not read off its `HARVEST-ORDER` is
not a worse figure, it is a measurement of the knob.

That also disposes of the reading that `rows_rev` is a "better" or "worse"
harvest. It is not: its own pair floor is 317 where the forward pair's is 0, so
it is simply a noisier plane to measure in, and both planes agree about us.

**What this means for the published number.** `PARITY.md` §3 records that two
vanilla runs in different orders differ by *"253 in the End"*. That was an r=7
figure; at r=16 it is 944, and 100% of both live in one biome that is a quarter
of the window. An End exactness quoted without its biome mix is a weighted
average whose weights nobody stated — and in the End's case the weight on
`the_end` is zero, so it is an average that excludes the island the dimension
is named for. `ci/end-biome-window.sh` is the artefact that states the weights.

## 50. The End's 100.000000% has never been asked about the one thing the port does not do (2026-08-18)

`worldgen-assembly`'s own caveat block names `end_gateway_return` as the single
unported End feature, and that is still true. What was never stated is that
**no measured window contains one**, and that this is arithmetic rather than
bad luck.

The three windows, censused for it directly:

```text
  c(64,64) r=16  1,089 chunks   0 end_gateway,  0 bedrock
  c(83,62) r=7     225 chunks   0 end_gateway,  0 bedrock
  c(0,0)   r=7     225 chunks   0 end_gateway, 10 bedrock  <-- the ten spike tops
```
